Lecture Notes on Edge Universality for Random Regular Graphs
Horng-Tzer Yau

Abstract
The purpose of this note is to explain the structure, general strategy, and main ideas of the proof in the work of Huang, McKenzie, and Yau (2024) on the Ramanujan property and edge universality of random regular graphs. The core of the argument is the derivation of self-consistent equations and a microscopic version of the loop equations for random d d -regular graphs. We first recall the local law for random d d -regular graphs, and then illustrate the main ideas behind the derivation of the self-consistent equations and the first loop equation.



University of Pennsylvania

huangjy@wharton.upenn.edu

Harvard University

htyau@math.harvard.edu

1
Introduction

We give an exposition of the ideas and proofs in
[
15
]
.
The overall strategy consists of three main components:

1.

proving concentration for the self-consistent equations, via estimates of
the expectations of high moments of these equations, leading to optimal
concentration of eigenvalues;

2.

proving a microscopic version of the loop equations, which can be viewed
as a refinement of the self-consistent equations with an additional correction
term;

3.

deriving edge universality using the loop equations together with Dyson
Brownian motion.

We start in
Section

2
by recalling some useful identities for the Green’s
function, which are standard tools in random matrix theory. In

Section

3
, we recall the local law for random
d
-regular graphs and
related concepts from the previous work
[
17
]
.

To illustrate the basic ideas, in this note we only prove estimates for the
expectation (first moment) of the self-consistent equations and derive the
first microscopic version of the loop equation. These are stated in

Theorem

4.5
. We then derive edge universality using the loop equations
in
Section

4
. The proof of
Theorem

4.5
occupies the
remainder of the note.

The key tool is the local resampling procedure in
Section

5
,
which efficiently exploits the randomness of random
d
-regular graphs. Local
resampling produces an exchangeable pair of random
d
-regular graphs (before
and after resampling). In
Section

6
, we explain the basic ideas of
proving concentration using exchangeable pairs, and in
Section

7
we discuss
how to apply this method in the setting of random
d
-regular graphs. It
turns out, however, that the resulting error is too weak and far from optimal.

To derive self-consistent equations with optimal error, we need to:

1.

develop an iteration mechanism, in which we repeatedly perform local
resampling and the error improves at each step (see
Section

11
);

2.

track the terms arising from the iteration by introducing admissible
functions and forests in
Section

8
;

3.

for terms depending on the graph after local resampling, rewrite them as
functions of the original graph using either the Schur complement formula (in

Section

9
) or the Woodbury formula (in
Section

10
);

4.

control the errors generated at each iteration step, as done in

Section

13
.

We remark that the derivation of the estimates for the self-consistent
equations and the loop equation are not independent. The loop equation (as
proved in
Section

12
) essentially follows from identifying the next
order correction to the self-consistent equations.

1.1
Random
d
-regular graphs

A graph on
N
vertices
[\![{N}]\!]=\{1,\dots,N\}
is
d
-regular if every vertex has degree
d
.
Necessarily
1\leqslant d\leqslant N-1
and
dN
is even. In this article we mainly consider the
uniform model

{{\mathcal{G}}_{N,d}}
:
Sample uniformly from the finite set of all
simple
(no loops or multiple edges)
d
-regular graphs on
N
vertices
[\![{N}]\!]
.

Besides the uniform model, several other models are considered in the literature.

Permutation model.

When
d
is even, take
d/2
independent uniformly distributed permutations
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d/2}
on
[\![{N}]\!]
.
The permutation model is the random graph on
N
vertices obtained by adding an edge
\{i,\sigma_{j}(i)\}
for each
i\in[\![{N}]\!]

and
1\leqslant j\leqslant d/2
.

Matching model.

When
N
is even, take
d
independent uniformly distributed perfect matchings
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d}
on
[\![{N}]\!]
.
The matching model is the random graph on
N
vertices obtained by adding an edge
\{i,\sigma_{j}(i)\}
for each
i\in[\![{N}]\!]

and
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
.

These models yield a
d
-regular
multigraph
with possible self-loops and
multiple edges; conditioning on simplicity gives the uniform model
{\mathcal{G}}_{N,d}
. In this article we mainly discuss the uniform model, but many results also hold for other models.

We can identify a
d
-regular graph with its adjacency matrix
A
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be a simple, undirected random graph on the vertex set
[\![{N}]\!]
.
Its adjacency matrix
A=(A_{ij})\in\{0,1\}^{N\times N}
is defined by
A_{ij}=1
if and only if
i
and
j
are adjacent. For
d
-regular graphs, it is immediate that
A
has a trivial eigenvalue
d
with associated eigenvector
{\bm{e}}=(1,1,\dots,1)^{\top}
. Moreover, by the Perron-Frobenius theorem, all other eigenvalues are bounded in absolute value by
d
. For convenience, we shall consider the normalized adjacency matrix

H\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=A/\sqrt{d-1}.

(1.1)

We denote the eigenvalues of the normalized adjacency matrix
H
of a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
on
N
vertices as
\lambda_{1}=d/\sqrt{d-1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}
. We also introduce the Green’s function and the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue distribution of the normalized adjacency matrix
H

\displaystyle G(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(H-z)^{-1},\quad m_{N}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}G_{ii}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}-z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}.

(1.2)

By local weak convergence, the empirical eigenvalue density of random
d
-regular graphs converges to that of the infinite
d
-regular tree, which is known as the Kesten-McKay distribution; see
[
19
,
21
]
. This density is given by

\displaystyle\varrho_{d}(x):=\mathbf{1}_{x\in[-2,2]}\left(1+\frac{1}{d-1}-\frac{x^{2}}{d}\right)^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2\pi}.

(1.3)

Note that close to the spectral edge
\pm 2
, the Kesten-Mckay distribution has square root behavior:

\displaystyle x\rightarrow\pm 2,\quad\varrho_{d}(x)=\frac{{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{2\mp x}}{\pi}+\OO(|2\mp x|),\quad{\mathcal{A}}:=\frac{d(d-1)}{(d-2)^{2}}.

(1.4)

We denote by
m_{d}(z)
the Stieltjes transform of the Kesten–McKay distribution
\varrho_{d}(x)
,

\displaystyle m_{d}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varrho_{d}(x){\rm d}x}{x-z}=(d-1)\frac{-(d-2)z+d\sqrt{z^{2}-4}}{2(d^{2}-(d-1)z^{2})},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}:=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\vcentcolon\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]>0\}.

We recall the semicircle distribution
\varrho_{\rm sc}(x)
and its Stieltjes transform
m_{\rm sc}(z)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varrho_{\rm sc}(x)=\bm{1}_{x\in[-2,2]}\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2\pi},\quad m_{\rm sc}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varrho_{\rm sc}(x){\rm d}x}{x-z}=\frac{-z+\sqrt{z^{2}-4}}{2}.\end{split}

(1.5)

Explicitly, the Stieltjes transform of the Kesten–McKay distribution
m_{d}(z)
can be expressed in terms of the Stieltjes transform
m_{\rm sc}(z)
:

\displaystyle m_{d}(z)=\frac{1}{-z-\frac{d}{d-1}m_{\rm sc}(z)}.

(1.6)

1.2
Main Results

Our main result in
[
15
]
verifies the edge universality conjecture for random
d
-regular graphs by Sarnak
[
24
]
and Miller, Novikoff and Sabelli
[
23
]
.

Theorem 1.1

(Edge eigenvalue universality
[
15
]
)
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
,
k\geqslant 1
and
s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
{\mathcal{A}}=d(d-1)/(d-2)^{2}
from (
1.4
). There exists a small
\varepsilon>0
such that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}={d}/{\sqrt{d-1}}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}
of the normalized adjacency matrix
H
of random
d
-regular graphs satisfy:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}_{H}\left(({\mathcal{A}}N)^{2/3}(\lambda_{2}-2)\geqslant s_{1},\cdots,({\mathcal{A}}N)^{2/3}(\lambda_{k+1}-2)\geqslant s_{k}\right)\\
&=\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{GOE}}\left(N^{2/3}(\mu_{1}-2)\geqslant s_{1},\cdots,N^{2/3}(\mu_{k}-2)\geqslant s_{k}\right)+\OO(N^{-\varepsilon}),\end{split}

where
\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\mu_{N}
are the eigenvalues of the GOE.
The analogous statement holds for the smallest eigenvalues
-\lambda_{N},\ldots,-\lambda_{N-k+1}
.

When the degree
d
grows with the size of the graph, edge universality for random
d
-regular graphs has been established previously for
N^{2/3+\oo(1)}\leqslant d\leqslant N/2
, by He
[
14
]
, and for
N^{\oo(1)}\leqslant d\leqslant N^{1/3-\oo(1)}
, by the authors of this paper
[
16
]
, which generalized a result for
N^{2/9+\oo(1)}\leqslant d\leqslant N^{1/3-\oo(1)}
, by Bauerschmidt, Knowles and the authors of this paper
[
2
]
.

Theorem

1.1
implies that the fluctuation of the second largest eigenvalue converges to the Tracy-Widom
1
distribution. The Tracy-Widom
1
distribution has about
83\%
of its mass on the set
\{x:x<0\}
[
11
]
.
Therefore
Theorem

1.1
implies
83\%
of

d
-regular graphs have the second eigenvalue
less than

2
.
The proof of
Theorem

1.1
can be extended to show
that the largest and smallest nontrivial eigenvalues converge in distribution to
independent
Tracy-Widom
1
distributions.
As a consequence, we have the following result.

Corollary 1.2

(
[
15
]
)
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
and
N
sufficiently large. With probability approximately
69\%
, a randomly sampled
d
-regular graph has
\max\{\lambda_{2},|\lambda_{N}|\}\leqslant 2
, and is therefore Ramanujan.

1.3
Parameters

In this note we fix the parameters as follows

\displaystyle 0<{\mathfrak{o}}\ll{\mathfrak{b}}\ll{\mathfrak{c}}\ll{\mathfrak{g}}\ll 1,

(1.7)

set
{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{c}}/4)\log_{d-1}N
and choose
\ell
such that
\ell/\log_{d-1}N\ll{\mathfrak{b}}
.
Below, we describe their meanings and where they are introduced:

•

Many estimates involve bounds containing
N^{\mathfrak{o}}
factors, which are harmless.

•

{\mathfrak{b}}
relates to the concentration of Green’s function entries, with errors bounded by
N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
, see (
3.20
) and (
3.21
).

•

For the spectral parameter
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
in Green’s functions and Stieltjes transforms, we restrict it to
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}
, see (
1.8
).

•

\ell
comes from local resampling in
Section

5
, we resample boundary edges of balls with radius
\ell
.

•

{\mathfrak{c}}
defines
{\mathfrak{R}}
, and with high probability, random
d
-regular graphs are tree-like within radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhoods, see
Definition

3.1
.

We restrict our analysis to the spectral domain

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}:N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{o}}},|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]|\leqslant 2+N^{-{\mathfrak{o}}}\}.

(1.8)

In the spectral domain
\bf D
, we impose the conditions
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{o}}}
and
|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]|\leqslant 2+N^{-{\mathfrak{o}}}
. These constraints ensure that
|m_{\rm sc}(z)|
is close to
1
, specifically satisfying
||m_{\rm sc}(z)|-1|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{o}}/2}
.

2
Properties of the Green’s functions

Throughout this paper, we repeatedly use some (well-known) identities for Green’s functions,
which we collect them here.

2.1
Resolvent identity

The following well-known identity is referred as resolvent identity:
for two invertible matrices
A
and
B
of the same size, we have

A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}=B^{-1}(B-A)A^{-1}.

(2.1)

2.2
Woodbury formula

Let
A+UCV^{\top}
be a rank
r
perturbation of
A
. Namely,
U,V\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times r}
and
C\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times r}
. Then, the Woodbury formula gives us

\displaystyle(A+UCV^{\top})^{-1}-A^{-1}=-A^{-1}U(C^{-1}+V^{\top}A^{-1}U)^{-1}V^{\top}A^{-1}.

(2.2)

2.3
Schur complement formula

Given an
N\times N
matrix
H
and an index set
\mathbb{T}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
, recall that we denote by

H|_{\mathbb{T}}
the
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}
-matrix obtained by restricting
H
to
\mathbb{T}
,
and that by
H^{(\mathbb{T})}=H|_{\mathbb{T}^{\complement}}
the matrix obtained by removing
the rows and columns corresponding to indices in
\mathbb{T}
.
Thus, for any
\mathbb{T}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
,
any symmetric matrix
H
can be written (up to rearrangement of indices) in the block form

H=\begin{bmatrix}A&B^{\top}\\
B&D\end{bmatrix},

with
A=H|_{\mathbb{T}}
and
D=H^{(\mathbb{T})}
.
The Schur complement formula asserts that, for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
,

G=(H-z)^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}(A-B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}B)^{-1}&-(A-B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}B)^{-1}B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}\\
-G^{(\mathbb{T})}B(A-B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}B)^{-1}&G^{(\mathbb{T})}+G^{(\mathbb{T})}B(A-B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}B)^{-1}B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}\end{bmatrix},

(2.3)

where
G^{(\mathbb{T})}=(D-z)^{-1}
.
Throughout the paper, we often use the following special cases of (
2.3
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}G|_{\mathbb{T}}&=(A-B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})}B)^{-1},\\
G|_{\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\complement}}&=-G|_{\mathbb{T}}B^{\top}G^{(\mathbb{T})},\\
G|_{\mathbb{T}^{\complement}}&=G^{(\mathbb{T})}+G|_{\mathbb{T}^{\complement}\mathbb{T}}(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}G|_{\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\complement}}=G^{(\mathbb{T})}-G^{(\mathbb{T})}BG|_{\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\complement}},\end{split}

(2.4)

as well as the special case

G_{ij}^{(k)}=G_{ij}-\frac{G_{ik}G_{kj}}{G_{kk}}=G_{ij}+(G^{(k)}H)_{ik}G_{kj}.

(2.5)

2.4
Ward identity

For any symmetric
N\times N
matrix
H
, its Green’s function
G(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
satisfies
the Ward identity

\sum_{j=1}^{N}|G_{ij}(z)|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}G_{jj}(z)}{\eta},

(2.6)

where
\eta=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]
. This can be deduced from using (
2.1
) on
G-G^{*}
. This identity provides a bound for the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{N}|G_{ij}(z)|^{2}

in terms of the diagonal entries of the Green’s function.

3
Local law for random
d
-regular graphs

In this section, we present the local law results from
[
17
]
. It states that the Green’s function of random
d
-regular graphs are approximated by the Green’s function extension with general weights, which are easy to compute. We introduce the concept of Green’s function extension with general weights in
Section

3.2
, and state the local law in
Section

3.3
.

3.1
Locally tree-like graphs

Random
d
-regular graphs are locally tree-like in the sense that each radius
\OO(\log_{d-1}(N))
neighborhoods is either a truncated tree (contains no cycles), or contains at most one cycle.

Definition 3.1
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
and a sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{c}}<1
,
{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{c}}/4)\log_{d-1}N
as in (
1.7
). We define the event
\overline{\Omega}
, where the following occur:

1.

The number of vertices that do not have a tree neighborhood of radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
is at most
N^{{\mathfrak{c}}}
.

2.

The radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhood of each vertex has an excess (i.e., the number of independent cycles) of at most
1
.

The event
\overline{\Omega}
is a typical event. The following proposition from
[
17
, Proposition 2.1]
states that
\overline{\Omega}
holds with high probability.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
17
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

\overline{\Omega}
occurs with probability
1-\OO(N^{-(1-{\mathfrak{c}})})
.

As we will see, for graphs
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, their Green’s functions can be approximated by tree extensions with overwhelmingly high probability.
For the infinite
d
-regular tree and the infinite
(d-1)
-ary tree (trees where the root has degree
d-1
and all other vertices have degree
d
),
the following proposition computes their Green’s function explicitly.

Proposition 3.3

(
[
17
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be the infinite
d
-regular tree.
For all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
, its Green’s function is

G_{ij}(z)=m_{d}(z)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{X}}}(i,j)},

(3.1)

where
\dist_{\mathcal{X}}(i,j)
is the graph distance of the two vertices
i,j
in
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be the infinite
(d-1)
-ary tree with root vertex
o
.
Its Green’s function is

G_{ij}(z)=m_{d}(z)\left(1-\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2{\rm anc}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(i,j)+2}\right)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(i,j)},

(3.2)

where
{\rm anc}_{\mathcal{Y}}(i,j)
is the distance from the common ancestor of the vertices
i,j
to the root
o
.
In particular,

\displaystyle G_{oi}(z)=m_{\rm sc}(z)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(o,i)}.

(3.3)

3.2
Green’s function extension with general weights

We recall the notion of a Green’s function extension with general weight
\Delta
from
[
17
, Section 2.3]
. Roughly speaking, we consider the radius-
\ell
neighborhood
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
of a vertex
o
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Assume it is a tree, then the boundary vertices of
{\mathcal{T}}
are leaves (i.e., vertices of degree 1), to each of which we attach a weight
-\Delta
; see the left panel of
Figure

1
. The resulting Green’s function of
{\mathcal{T}}
with these boundary weights is referred to as the Green’s function extension with general weight
\Delta
. The following formal definition slightly extend this idea to allow cycles.

Definition 3.4
.

Fix degree
d\geqslant 3
, and a graph
{\mathcal{T}}
with degrees bounded by
d
. We define the function
P({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta)
as follows. We denote
A({\mathcal{T}})
the adjacency matrix of
{\mathcal{T}}
,
D({\mathcal{T}})
the diagonal matrix of degrees of
{\mathcal{T}}
, and
\mathbb{I}
the diagonal matrix indexed by the vertex set
\mathbb{T}
of
{\mathcal{T}}
. Then

\displaystyle P({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta):=\frac{1}{-z+A({\mathcal{T}})/\sqrt{d-1}-(d\mathbb{I}-D({\mathcal{T}}))\Delta/(d-1)}.

(3.4)

The matrix
P({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta)
is the Green’s function of the matrix obtained from
A({\mathcal{T}})/\sqrt{d-1}
by attaching to each vertex
i\in{\mathcal{T}}
a weight
-(d-D_{ii}({\mathcal{T}}))\Delta/(d-1)
, see the middle panel of
Figure

1
(
d=3
and one boundary vertex has degree
2
). When
\Delta=m_{\rm sc}(z)
, (
3.4
) is the Green’s function of the tree extension of
{\mathcal{T}}
, i.e. extending
{\mathcal{T}}
by attaching copies of infinite
(d-1)
-ary trees to
{\mathcal{T}}
to make each vertex degree
d
. If
{\mathcal{T}}
is a tree, then in this case, the Green’s function agrees with the Green’s function of the infinite
d
-regular tree, as in (
3.1
).
For any vertex set
{\mathbb{X}}
in
{\mathcal{T}}
, we define the following Green’s function with vertex
{\mathbb{X}}
removed, see the right panel of
Figure

1
. Let
A^{({\mathbb{X}})}({\mathcal{T}})
and
D^{({\mathbb{X}})}({\mathcal{T}})
denote the matrices obtained from
A({\mathcal{T}}),D({\mathcal{T}})
by removing the row and column associated with vertex
{\mathbb{X}}
, and
\mathbb{I}
the diagonal matrix indexed by the vertex set
\mathbb{T}\setminus{\mathbb{X}}
. The Green’s function is then defined as:

\displaystyle P^{({\mathbb{X}})}({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta):=\frac{1}{-z+A^{({\mathbb{X}})}({\mathcal{T}})/\sqrt{d-1}-(d\mathbb{I}-D^{({\mathbb{X}})}({\mathcal{T}}))\Delta/(d-1)},

(3.5)

\mathcal{T}
o
i
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta

\mathcal{T}
o
i
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\Delta/2}
-\Delta

\mathcal{T}^{(i)}
o
i
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta
-\Delta

Figure 1
:
Left panel: for a truncated
d
-regular tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, we attach a weight
-\Delta
to each leaf vertex; middle panel: for a general graph
{\mathcal{T}}
, we attach a weight
-(d-D_{ii}({\mathcal{T}}))/(d-1)
to each vertex; right panel: remove the vertex set
{\mathbb{X}}=\{i\}
.

For any integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, we define the functions
X_{\ell}(\Delta,z),Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)
as

\displaystyle X_{\ell}(\Delta,z)=P_{oo}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{X}}),z,\Delta),\quad Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)=P_{oo}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{Y}}),z,\Delta),

(3.6)

where
{\mathcal{X}}
is the infinite
d
-regular tree with root vertex
o
, and
{\mathcal{Y}}
is the infinite
(d-1)
-ary tree with root vertex
o
.
Then
m_{\rm sc}(z)
is a fixed point of the function
Y_{\ell}
, i.e.
Y_{\ell}(m_{\rm sc}(z),z)=m_{\rm sc}(z)
. Also,
X_{\ell}(m_{\rm sc}(z),z)=m_{d}(z)
. The following proposition states that if
\Delta
is sufficiently close to
m_{\rm sc}(z)
, then
Y_{\ell}(\Delta,w)
is close to
m_{\rm sc}(z)
, and
X_{\ell}(\Delta,w)
is close to
m_{d}(z)
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Given
z,\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
such that
\ell^{2}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z)|,\ell^{2}|\Delta^{\prime}-m_{\rm sc}(z)|\ll 1
, then
the functions
X_{\ell}(\Delta,z),Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)

satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)&=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)m_{d}(z)\left(\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{d-1}+\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z)}\right)(\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}\\
&+m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)(\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO(\ell^{5}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{3}).\end{split}

(3.7)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}X_{\ell}(\Delta,z)-m_{d}(z)&=\frac{d}{d-1}m_{d}^{2}(z)m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell}(z)(\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO\left(|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\right).\end{split}

(3.8)

Moreover, the difference
Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)-Y_{\ell}(\Delta^{\prime},z)
satisfies

\displaystyle|Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)-Y_{\ell}(\Delta^{\prime},z)|\lesssim\ell|\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}|,

(3.9)

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

The proofs for
X_{\ell}
and
Y_{\ell}
are identical, so we will only provide the proof for
Y_{\ell}
.
We denote
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{Y}})
, which is the truncated
(d-1)
-ary tree at level
\ell
. We denote its vertex set as
\mathbb{H}
and normalized adjacency matrix as
H
. We denote
\mathbb{I}
and
\mathbb{I}^{\partial}
the diagonal matrices, such that for
x,y\in\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{I}_{xy}=\delta_{xy}
and
\mathbb{I}^{\partial}_{xy}=\bm{1}(\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(x,o)=\ell)\delta_{xy}
. Then (
3.4
) gives that

\displaystyle Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)=P({\mathcal{H}},z,\Delta)=\left(H-z-\Delta\mathbb{I}^{\partial}\right)^{-1},\quad P=P({\mathcal{H}},z,m_{\rm sc}(z))=(H-z-m_{\rm sc}(z)\mathbb{I}^{\partial})^{-1}.

In the rest of the proof, we will simply write
m_{\rm sc}=m_{\rm sc}(z)
. We can compute the Green’s function
P({\mathcal{H}},z,\Delta)
by a perturbation argument,

\displaystyle\begin{split}P({\mathcal{H}},z,\Delta)&=\left(H-z-\Delta\mathbb{I}^{\partial}\right)^{-1}=\left(H-z-m_{\rm sc}\mathbb{I}^{\partial}-(\Delta-m_{\rm sc})\mathbb{I}^{\partial})\right)^{-1}\\
&=P+\sum_{k\geqslant 1}(\Delta-m_{\rm sc})^{k}P(\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P)^{k}.\end{split}

(3.10)

With the explicit expression of
P
as given in (
3.3
), we can compute

\displaystyle\left(P\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P\right)_{oo}=\sum_{l}P_{ol}P_{lo}=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}m_{\rm sc}^{2}\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2\ell}

\displaystyle=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}

(3.11)

where the summation is over
l,l^{\prime}
such that
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,o)=\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l^{\prime},o)=\ell
, and there are
(d-1)^{\ell}
values of
l
. Moreover, for
k\geqslant 2
we will show the following two relations for
P
,

\displaystyle\left(P\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P\right)_{oo}=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}m_{d}\left(\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}}{d-1}+\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}}{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}}\right),

(3.12)

\displaystyle(|P|^{k})_{oo}\lesssim(C\ell)^{2k-3},

(3.13)

where for each
i,j\in\mathbb{H}
,
|P|_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=|P_{ij}|
.

The relation (
3.12
) follows from explicit computation using (
3.2
) and (
3.3
)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}P_{ol}P_{ll^{\prime}}P_{l^{\prime}o}=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}m_{\rm sc}^{2}\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2\ell}m_{d}\left(1-\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2+2{\rm anc}_{\mathcal{H}}(l,l^{\prime})}\right)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,l^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}m_{d}\left(1-\left(\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2+2{\rm anc}_{\mathcal{H}}(l,l^{\prime})}\right)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,l^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}m_{d}\left(1-\left(\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2+2\ell}+\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\left(1-\left(\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2+2(\ell-r)}\right)\left(\frac{m_{\rm sc}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{2r}(d-2)(d-1)^{r-1}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}m_{d}\left(\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}}{d-1}+\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}}{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}}\right),

where the summation in the first line is over
l,l^{\prime}
such that
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,o)=\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l^{\prime},o)=\ell
; in the second to last line we used that there are
(d-1)^{\ell}
choices of
l
; for a given
l
, there are
(d-2)(d-1)^{r-1}
values of
l^{\prime}
such that
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,l^{\prime})=2r,{\rm anc}_{\mathcal{H}}(l,l^{\prime})=\ell-r
for
1\leqslant r\leqslant\ell
. When
l=l^{\prime}
, it holds
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(l,l^{\prime})=0,{\rm anc}_{\mathcal{H}}(l,l^{\prime})=\ell
.

To prove (
3.13
), we show by induction that for any vertex
i
such that
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)\leqslant\ell
,

\displaystyle(|P|^{k})_{oi}\lesssim\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-2}}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)/2}}.

(3.14)

The statement for
k=1
follows from (
3.2
). Assume the statement (
3.14
) holds for
k-1
, we prove it for
k
,

\displaystyle(|P|^{k})_{oi}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{H}}(|P|^{k-1})_{oj}|P|_{ji}\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{H}}\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-4}}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,j)/2}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,i)/2}}.

(3.15)

We denote the path from
o
to
i
as
{\mathcal{P}}
, then
|\{j\in\mathbb{H}:\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,{\mathcal{P}})=r\}|\leqslant\ell(d-1)^{r}
, and for
\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,{\mathcal{P}})=r
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,j)/2}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,i)/2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{(d-1)^{r}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)/2}}.

In this way, we can reorganize the sum over
j
in (
3.15
) according to its distance to
{\mathcal{P}}
,

\displaystyle(|P|^{k})_{oi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\ell}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{H}:\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,{\mathcal{P}})=r}\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-4}}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,j)/2}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,i)/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r=0}^{\ell}|\{j:\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(j,{\mathcal{P}})=r\}|\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-4}}{(d-1)^{r}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)/2}}\lesssim\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-2}}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)/2}},

which shows (
3.14
). The claim (
3.13
) is a consequence of (
3.14
)

\displaystyle(|P|^{k})_{oo}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{H}}(|P|^{k-1})_{oi}|P|_{io}\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{H}}\frac{(C\ell)^{2k-4}}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(o,i)/2}}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{H}}}(i,o)/2}}\lesssim(C\ell)^{2k-3}.

The claim (
3.7
) follows from plugging (
3.11
), (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) into (
3.10
), and use
\ell^{2}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}|\ll 1
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)&=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}(\Delta-m_{\rm sc})^{k}(P(\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P)^{k})_{oo}\\
&=m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)m_{d}(z)\left(\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{d-1}+\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z)}\right)(\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}\\
&+m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)(\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\sum_{k\geqslant 3}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{k}(|P|^{k+1})_{oo}.\end{split}

(3.16)

The claim (
3.9
) follows from bounding the derivative

\displaystyle|\partial_{1}Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)|\lesssim\ell.

(3.17)

The claim (
3.17
) follows from taking derivative with respect to
\Delta
on both sides of (
3.16
), and plugging in (
3.11
) and (
3.13
)

\displaystyle|\partial_{1}Y_{\ell}(\Delta,z)|\lesssim|(P\mathbb{I}^{\partial}P)_{oo}|+\sum_{k\geqslant 1}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}|^{k}(|P|^{k+2})_{oo}\lesssim 1+C\ell\sum_{k\geqslant 1}(C\ell^{2}|\Delta-m_{\rm sc}|)^{k}\lesssim\ell.

∎

3.3
Local law of
H

To state the local law of
H
, we introduce a quantity
Q(z)
that was first defined in
[
3
,
17
]
, and plays a crucial role in the proof of local law. The quantity is the average of
G_{jj}^{(i)}(z)
over all pairs of adjacent vertices
i\sim j
:

\displaystyle Q(z):=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim j}G_{jj}^{(i)}(z).

(3.18)

For any vertex set
{\mathbb{X}}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
, and integer
r\geqslant 1
, we denote

{\mathcal{B}}_{r}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathcal{G}})=\{i\in[\![{N}]\!]:\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(i,{\mathbb{X}})\leqslant r\}
the ball of radius-
r
around vertices
{\mathbb{X}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.
The weak local law of
H
has been proven in
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
, which is recalled below (by taking
({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}},{\mathfrak{c}},\mathfrak{r})=(12,300,{\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}/32)
).

Theorem 3.6

(
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Fix any sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{b}}\ll{\mathfrak{c}}<1
,
{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{c}}/4)\log_{d-1}(N)
and any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
, we define
\eta(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z],\kappa(z)=\min\{|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]-2|,|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]+2|\}
, and the error parameters

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}(z):=(\log N)^{100}\left(N^{-10{\mathfrak{b}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{d}(z)]}{N\eta(z)}}+\frac{1}{(N\eta(z))^{2/3}}\right),\quad\varepsilon(z):=\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)}{\sqrt{\kappa(z)+\eta(z)+\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)}}.

(3.19)

For any
{\mathfrak{C}}\geqslant 1
and
N
large enough, with probability at least
1-\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{C}}})
with respect to the uniform measure on
\overline{\Omega}
,

|G_{ij}(z)-P_{ij}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\{i,j\},{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{\rm sc}(z))|,\quad|Q(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)|,\quad|m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z)|\lesssim\varepsilon(z)

(3.20)

uniformly for every
i,j\in[\![{N}]\!]
, and any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant(\log N)^{300}/N
.

Definition 3.7
.

We denote by
\Omega\subset\overline{\Omega}
the set of
d
-regular graphs
for which (
3.20
) holds.

As a consequence of
Theorem

3.6
we have the following statement.

Claim 3.8
.

Uniformly for every
i,j\in[\![{N}]\!]
, and any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
with
|z|\leqslant 1/{\mathfrak{g}},\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}
, we have the following bounds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|G_{ij}(z)-P_{ij}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\{i,j\},{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{\rm sc}(z)|\lesssim N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}},\\
&|Q(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)|,\quad|m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z)|\lesssim N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}}.\end{split}

(3.21)

and for any
i,j\in[\![{N}]\!]

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{ij}(z)]\lesssim N^{{\mathfrak{o}}/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]

(3.22)

Proof.

For
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}
, thanks to (
3.19
),

\displaystyle\varepsilon(z)\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)}\leqslant N^{-4{\mathfrak{b}}}.

(3.23)

Thus, the claim (
3.21
) follows from (
3.20
).

We denote the eigenvectors of
H
as
{\bm{u}}_{1},{\bm{u}}_{2},\cdots,{\bm{u}}_{N}
. We notice that the first statement in (
3.20
) implies that for
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant(\log N)^{300}/N
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{ii}(z)]\lesssim 1
. It follows that eigenvectors are delocalized

\displaystyle\|{\bm{u}}_{\alpha}\|^{2}_{\infty}\lesssim\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}((\log N)^{300}/N)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{ii}(\lambda_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}(\log N)^{300}/N)]\lesssim(\log N)^{300}/N\ll N^{{\mathfrak{o}}/2-1}.

Thus (
3.22
) follows

\displaystyle|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xy}(z)]|=\left|\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\frac{\eta({\bm{u}}_{\alpha}{\bm{u}}^{\top}_{\alpha})_{xy}}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|^{2}}\right|\leqslant N^{{\mathfrak{o}}/2-1}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\frac{\eta}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|^{2}}=N^{{\mathfrak{o}}/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)].

(3.24)

∎

The following lemma states that after removing some vertices of
{\mathcal{G}}
, the local law (
3.21
) still holds (with possibly worse error).

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
satisfy
|z|\leqslant 1/{\mathfrak{g}}
, and set
\eta:=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Fix a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
(recall
Definition

3.7
) and vertices

\{i,o\}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
with vertex set
\mathbb{T}
. For any
0\leqslant\mu\lesssim(d-1)^{\ell}
, take a family of directed edges

\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, set

\mathbb{W}=\{b_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, and condition on the event that

\displaystyle A_{io}\prod_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}A_{b_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}\prod_{x\neq y\in\{o,c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\mu}\}}\bm{1}\bigl({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(x,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is a tree}\bigr)\bm{1}\bigl(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(x,y)\geqslant{\mathfrak{R}}/2\bigr)=1.

(3.25)

Then, for any
{\mathbb{X}}
of the form

{\mathbb{X}}\in\{\mathbb{W},\,\mathbb{T}\cup\mathbb{W}\}
and any
x,y\not\in{\mathbb{X}}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|G^{({\mathbb{X}})}_{xy}(z)-P^{({\mathbb{X}})}_{xy}\bigl({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\{x,y\}\cup{\mathbb{X}},{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{\rm sc}(z)\bigr)\bigr|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}},\qquad\bigl|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}G^{({\mathbb{X}})}_{xy}(z)\bigr|\lesssim N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)],

(3.26)

and for any
x\not\in{\mathbb{X}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{b\sim c\notin{\mathbb{X}}}\bigl|G_{cx}^{({\mathbb{X}})}(z)\bigr|^{2}\lesssim\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}m_{N}(z)}{N\eta},\qquad\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c\not\in{\mathbb{X}}}\bigl|G_{cx}^{(b{\mathbb{X}})}(z)\bigr|^{2}\lesssim\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]}{N\eta},

(3.27)

where the summation is over directed edges
(b,c)
of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and
b,c\not\in{\mathbb{X}}
.

Proof.

We will only prove the case that
{\mathbb{X}}=\mathbb{T}
, the other cases can be proven in the same way, so we omit.
From the Schur complement formula (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle G^{(\mathbb{T})}=G-G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}G.

(3.28)

We denote
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
and
P=P({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\mathbb{T}\cup\{x,y\},{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{\rm sc}(z))
. We recall that by (
3.25
) the vertex
o
has a tree neighborhood of radius
{\mathfrak{R}}/2
. The connected component component of
o
in

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\mathbb{T}\cup\{x,y\},{\mathcal{G}})
is a truncated
d
-regular tree.
As a consequence, when restricted to the connected component of
o
in

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\mathbb{T}\cup\{x,y\},{\mathcal{G}})
,
P
is the Green’s function
of the
d
-regular tree.

Then (
3.4
) gives
(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}=H|_{\mathbb{T}}-z-m_{\rm sc}(z)\mathbb{I}^{\partial}
, where the diagonal matrix
\mathbb{I}_{ij}^{\partial}=\bm{1}(\dist_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,i)=\ell)\delta_{ij}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{T}
. And for any
i\in\mathbb{T}
,

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}|\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|H_{ij}-z\delta_{ij}-\mathbb{I}_{ij}^{\partial}|\lesssim 1.

(3.29)

Moreover, if
x
is in the connected component of
o
in

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\mathbb{T}\cup\{x,y\},{\mathcal{G}})
, then
(
3.21
) implies

\displaystyle|G_{ij}-P_{ij}|,\quad|G_{xj}-P_{xj}|\leqslant N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{T}.

(3.30)

If
x
is not in the connected component of
o
in

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\mathbb{T}\cup\{x,y\},{\mathcal{G}})
, then
\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(x,j)>{\mathfrak{R}}/50
for any

j\in\mathbb{T}
. In this case
P_{xj}=0
, and by (
3.21
) we have

|G_{xj}|\leqslant N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}}
. In this case, we also have

\displaystyle|G_{ij}-P_{ij}|,\quad|G_{xj}-P_{xj}|\leqslant N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{T}.

(3.31)

We can perform a resolvent expansion according to (
2.1
) and rewrite
(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}
as: for some large enough
{\mathfrak{p}}\geqslant 1

\displaystyle\begin{split}(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}-(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\mathfrak{p}}(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\left((P|_{\mathbb{T}}-G|_{\mathbb{T}})(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\right)^{k}\right)_{ij}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\\
\lesssim\sum_{l\in\mathbb{T}}|((P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{il}|N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{T}}|((P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{lj}|+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\lesssim N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}},\end{split}

(3.32)

where in the second statement we used (
3.29
) and (
3.31
). From the above expression, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}|\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}|+\OO(|\mathbb{T}|N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}})\lesssim 1.

(3.33)

Again, since the vertex
o
has radius
{\mathfrak{R}}/2
tree neighborhood, for any
0\leqslant r\leqslant\ell
,
|\{j\in\mathbb{T}:\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(x,j)=r\}|\lesssim(d-1)^{r}
.
As a consequence, for any
i\in\mathbb{T}
, (
3.31
) and (
3.1
) together imply that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|P_{xj}|\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}\left(\frac{|m_{\rm sc}|}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,j)}\lesssim\sum_{0\leqslant r\leqslant 2\ell}\frac{|\{j\in\mathbb{T}:\dist(x,j)=r\}|}{(d-1)^{r/2}}\lesssim\ell,\\
&\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{xj}|\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|P_{xj}|+\OO(|\mathbb{T}|N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}})\lesssim\ell.\end{split}

(3.34)

For
P
, Schur complement formula (
2.4
) gives
P^{(\mathbb{T})}=P-P(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}P
. By taking difference with (
3.28
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}|G^{(\mathbb{T})}_{xy}-P^{(\mathbb{T})}_{xy}|\lesssim|G_{xy}-P_{xy}|+\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{xi}-P_{xi}||(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}||G_{jy}|\\
&+\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{T}}|P_{xi}||(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}-(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}||G_{jy}|+\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{T}}|P_{xi}||(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}||G_{jy}-P_{jy}|\\
&\lesssim|G_{xy}-P_{xy}|+N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}}\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{T}}\left(|(G)^{-1}_{ij}||G_{jy}|+|P_{xi}||G_{jy}|+|P_{xi}||(P|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ij}|\right)\\
&\lesssim\ell^{2}N^{2{\mathfrak{b}}}\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}.\end{split}

(3.35)

where in the second inequality, we used
(
3.31
) and (
3.32
); in the third inequality we used (
3.29
), (
3.34
) and (
3.33
). This finishes the proof of the first statement in (
3.26
).

By taking imaginary part on both sides of (
3.28
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xy}^{(\mathbb{T})}]&=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xy}]-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}G)_{xy}]\lesssim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xy}]+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xj}]|(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}G)_{jy}|\\
&+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|(G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{xj}||\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{jy}]|+\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{xj}|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{jk}]|G_{ky}|.\end{split}

(3.36)

In the following we show the following estimates

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}(|G_{xj}|+|(G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{xj}|+|G_{jy}|+|(G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{jy}|)\lesssim\ell,\quad\max_{j,k\in\mathbb{T}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{jk}]\lesssim\ell^{2}N^{{\mathfrak{o}}/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}].

(3.37)

Then the second statement in (
3.26
) follows from plugging (
3.24
) and (
3.37
) into (
3.36
).

We start with the first statement in (
3.37
).
The first statement in (
3.37
) follows from the estimates (
3.33
) and (
3.34
)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}(|G_{xj}|+|(G(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1})_{xj}|)\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{xj}|\left(1+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{T}}|(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{ji}|\right)\lesssim\ell.

Next we prove the second statement in (
3.37
). For the imaginary part of a symmetric matrix, we have the following identity

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[A^{-1}]=-A^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[A]\overline{A^{-1}}.

Thus

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}_{xy}]=-((G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G|_{\mathbb{T}}]\overline{(G|_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}})_{xy}.

(3.38)

The second statement in (
3.37
) follows from plugging (
3.24
) and (
3.33
) into (
3.38
).

The first statement in (
3.27
) follows from a Ward identity (
2.6
), and the second statement in (
3.26
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c\notin\mathbb{T}}|G_{cx}^{(\mathbb{T})}|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{xx}^{(\mathbb{T})}]}{N\eta}\lesssim\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}]}{N\eta}.

For the second statement in (
3.27
), thanks to the Schur complement formula (
2.5
),

\displaystyle|G_{cx}^{(b\mathbb{T})}|=\left|G_{cx}^{(\mathbb{T})}-\frac{G_{cb}^{(\mathbb{T})}G_{bx}^{(\mathbb{T})}}{G_{bb}^{(\mathbb{T})}}\right|\lesssim|G_{cx}^{(\mathbb{T})}|+|G_{bx}^{(\mathbb{T})}|,

(3.39)

where in the second statement we used (
3.26
) to bound
|G_{cb}^{(\mathbb{T})}|\lesssim 1,|G_{bb}^{(\mathbb{T})}|\gtrsim 1
. By averaging (
3.39
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c\not\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{cx}^{(b\mathbb{T})}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c\not\in\mathbb{T}}|G_{cx}^{(\mathbb{T})}|^{2}+|G_{bx}^{(\mathbb{T})}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}]}{N\eta},

where the second statement follows from the first statement in (
3.27
).

∎

4
Edge universality.

We now outline the ideas behind proving edge universality
Theorem

1.1
for random
d
-regular graphs. Edge universality follows the standard three-step scheme
[
12
]
. The key deviations arise in steps (i) and (iii):

(i)

In the sparse setting, the optimal concentration of eigenvalue locations is not with respect to the semicircle law as in the Wigner case. The self-consistent equations for the relevant Stieltjes transforms are therefore more intricate. New techniques—local resampling that more effectively exploit the randomness—are introduced to derive these equations and establish concentration; see
Section

4.1
for details.

(iii)

For random
d
-regular graphs with fixed
d
, standard Wigner-type comparison methods fail. A different mechanism based on loop equations plays a central role; see
Section

4.2
for details.

4.1
Three-step strategy.

In this section we recall the three-step strategy for proving edge universality for Wigner matrices (see, e.g.,
[
12
]
) and highlight the key differences that arise in random
d
-regular graph setting. Let
H
denote either a Wigner matrix or the normalized adjacency matrix of a random
d
-regular graph. We write its eigenvalues in decreasing order as
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}
.

Step (i): local law and rigidity.

The first step is to prove optimal eigenvalue rigidity up to the spectral edge (on the
N^{-2/3}
scale), yielding precise control of the extreme eigenvalues. This is achieved by deriving a self-consistent equation for the Stieltjes transform
m_{N}(z)
(or related spectral observables),

\displaystyle m_{N}(z):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}-z}.

For Wigner matrices, the self-consistent equation takes the form: with high probability

\displaystyle\,1+zm_{N}(z)+m_{N}^{2}(z)\approx 0,

(4.1)

whose unique solution is the Stieltjes transform of the semicircle law. The self-consistent equation is proven through carefully estimating high moments
\mathbb{E}[|1+zm_{N}(z)+m_{N}^{2}(z)|^{2p}]
.

For a random
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
on
N
vertices with fixed degree
d
,
obtaining a closed equation for the Stieltjes transform requires a more refined
observable. We recall from (
3.18
) the quantity

\displaystyle Q(z)=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim j}G_{jj}^{(i)}(z),

(4.2)

and the functions
Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z)
and
X_{\ell}(Q(z),z)
from (
3.6
),
which are the Green’s functions at the root of a truncated
(d-1)
-ary tree
and a truncated
d
-regular tree of depth
\ell\asymp\log_{d-1}N
with
boundary weights
Q(z)
. It turns out that the quantity
Q(z)
and the
Stieltjes transform
m_{N}(z)
satisfy two self-consistent equations:

\displaystyle Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z)\approx 0,\quad m_{N}(z)-X_{\ell}(Q(z),z)\approx 0.

(4.3)

One of the main results of
[
15
]
is devoted to proving the
self-consistent equations (
4.3
) via an iteration scheme
utilizing local resampling. This note is devoted to explaining the derivation
of these self-consistent equations by showing that their expectations are small.

Step (ii): strong ergodicity of Dyson Brownian motion.

We next study the normalized adjacency matrix perturbed by a small GOE matrix

\displaystyle H(t)\;\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\;H+\sqrt{t}\,Z,

(4.4)

where
Z
is a GOE matrix for Wigner matrices; for random
d
-regular graphs,
Z
is the GOE restricted to the subspace of matrices with vanishing row and column sums (so that the trivial eigenvector
{\bm{e}}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\top}
is preserved).

Assume the eigenvalues of
H
satisfy optimal edge rigidity with respect to a limiting density
\varrho
exhibiting square-root edge behavior, which is the consequence of Step (i). In particular, for Wigner matrices
\varrho
is the semicircle law (
1.5
); for random
d
-regular graphs
\varrho
is the Kesten–McKay law
\varrho_{d}
from (
1.3
).
Then, by
[
20
,
1
]
, after a time
t\geqslant N^{-1/3+{\mathfrak{c}}}
(for arbitrarily small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
) the edge statistics of
H(t)
are universal: the extreme eigenvalues converge to the Airy
1
point process (the edge scaling limit of GOE).

The asymptotic empirical eigenvalue distribution of the matrix
H(t)=H+\sqrt{t}Z
can be described by the free additive convolution, from free probability theory
[
5
]
. We denote the semicircle distribution of variance
t
as
t^{-1/2}\rho_{\text{sc}}(t^{-1/2}x)
.
Given a probability measure
\varrho
on
{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote its free convolution with a semicircle distribution of variance
t
by
\varrho_{t}:=\varrho\boxplus t^{-1/2}\rho_{\text{sc}}(t^{-1/2}x)
. Write the Stieltjes transforms of
\varrho
and
\varrho_{t}
as

\displaystyle m(z)=m(z;0)=\int(x-z)^{-1}\varrho(x){\rm d}x,\quad m(z;t)=\int(x-z)^{-1}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x,\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}.

The Stieltjes transform
m(z;t)
solves the complex Burgers equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}m(z,t)=\partial_{z}m(z,t)m(z,t)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}(m(z,t)^{2}).

(4.5)

We denote the right edge of the free convolution density
\varrho_{t}
as
E_{t}
, then its evolution satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E_{t}=m(E_{t},t).

(4.6)

Step (iii): Green’s function comparison.

This final step removes the Gaussian component in (
4.4
) and, via a comparison argument, transfers edge universality from the Gaussian-divisible model to the original ensemble. In particular, it shows that the
k
-point correlation function at the edge is invariant along the flow (
4.4
), with
t
interpreted as time. This is achieved by the following comparison theorem for multipoint correlation functions at the edge (equivalently, by comparing products of Stieltjes transforms on the microscopic scale). For details on how this theorem yields edge universality, see
[
12
, Section 17]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Fix a small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
. We introduce the microscopic window:

\displaystyle{\bf M}:=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:N^{-2/3-{\mathfrak{c}}}\leqslant|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]|\leqslant N^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{c}}},-N^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{c}}}\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[w]\leqslant N^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{c}}}\}.

(4.7)

Then for any
p\geqslant 1
, and
w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{p}\in{\bf M}
,
z_{j}(t)=E_{t}+w_{j}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant p
, the following holds

\displaystyle\left.\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}N^{1/3}(m_{N}(z_{j}(t);t)-m(z_{j}(t);t))\right]\right|_{t=0}^{t=N^{-1/3+{\mathfrak{c}}}}=\OO\left(N^{-{\mathfrak{c}}}\right),

(4.8)

provided
N
is sufficiently large.

For Wigner matrices and random graphs with polynomially growing degree,
Proposition

4.1
follows by integrating out the GOE perturbation
Z
via Gaussian integration by parts, expanding the randomness of
H
via cumulant expansion, and showing that the leading contributions cancel. For random
d
-regular graphs with fixed degree
d
, this argument breaks down. In the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, a key observation is that the time derivative of the multi-point correlation functions of the Stieltjes transform of
H(t)
is governed by microscopic loop equations. In the next section, we state these loop equations and explain how they yield the Green’s function comparison for fixed-degree
d
-regular graphs.

Figure 2
:
Extreme eigenvalues of Gaussian
\beta
-ensemble converge to the Airy
β
point process.

4.2
Loop equations and Green’s function comparison

Loop (or Dyson-Schwinger) equations were first used in the theoretical physics literature (e.g., in the work of Migdal
[
22
]
) and were later introduced to the mathematical community by Johansson
[
18
]
to derive macroscopic central limit theorems for general
\beta
-ensembles of eigenvalues of random matrices, see also
[
6
,
7
,
25
]
and the book
[
13
]
. For the Gaussian
\beta
-ensemble, the loop (or Dyson-Schwinger) equation describes a recursive structure satisfied by the Stieltjes transform (correlation functions) of the eigenvalues.
The first loop equation is given by: for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[m_{N}(z)^{2}+zm_{N}(z)+1+\frac{2-\beta}{\beta}\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z)}{N}\right]=0,\quad m_{N}(z):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}-z}.

(4.9)

More generally, for any
p\geqslant 1
, the loop equations at rank
p
are given by : for
z,z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{p-1}\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\left(m_{N}(z)^{2}+zm_{N}(z)+1+\frac{2-\beta}{\beta}\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z)}{N}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}m_{N}(z_{j})\right]\\
&+\frac{2}{\beta N^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z_{j}}\frac{m_{N}(z)-m_{N}(z_{j})}{z-z_{j}}\prod_{i\neq j}m_{N}(z_{i})\right]=0.\end{split}

(4.10)

These loop equations (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) arise from the invariance of the eigenvalue integral under infinitesimal reparametrizations of the variables; they can be derived either by integration by parts or, equivalently, by an infinitesimal change of variables.

More generally, one can derive the loop equations for
\beta
-ensembles with general potential
V

\displaystyle P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{N})=\frac{1}{Z_{N}^{\beta,V}}\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-\beta NV(\lambda_{i})}.

Loop (or Dyson-Schwinger) equations play a crucial role in proving eigenvalue rigidity and universality for
\beta
-ensembles with general potential
[
8
,
10
,
9
,
26
,
4
]
.

In general, loop equations are model-dependent. For
\beta
-ensembles, they depend on the potential
V
. However, when zooming in near the spectral edge, the microscopic version of loop equations becomes universal. To introduce
microscopic version of loop equations
, we recall that the extreme eigenvalues of GOE converge to the Airy
1
point process
\Lambda_{1}\geqslant\Lambda_{2}\geqslant\Lambda_{3}\geqslant\cdots
; see
Figure

2
. We can introduce the following normalized Stieltjes transform of the Airy
1
point process:

\displaystyle S(w)=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{i}-w}-\frac{1}{{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}}\right)-\frac{{\rm Ai}^{\prime}(0)}{{\rm Ai}(0)},\quad w\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}},

where
{\mathfrak{a}}_{1}>{\mathfrak{a}}_{2}>{\mathfrak{a}}_{3}>\cdots
are zeros of the Airy function
{\rm Ai}(w)
. Under the above normalization,
S(w)
has square root behavior:
S(w)\sim\sqrt{w}
as
w\rightarrow\infty
.
S(w)
can be viewed as a random meromorphic function, with poles given by the Airy
1
point process.

Formally, taking the soft-edge scaling limit of the loop equations (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), we obtain a hierarchy of equations for the Stieltjes transform
S(w)
of the Airy
1
point process. The rank-
p
loop equations are given by: for any
w,w_{1},\ldots,w_{p-1}\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left({(S(w)-\sqrt{w})^{2}+2\sqrt{w}(S(w)-\sqrt{w})+\partial_{w}S(w)}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}S(w_{i})\right]\\
&+2\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{w_{i}}\left(\frac{S(w)-S(w_{i})}{w-w_{i}}\right)\prod_{j:j\neq i}S(w_{j})\right]=0.\end{split}

(4.11)

These loop equations (
4.11
) provide necessary conditions for the edge statistics to converge to the Airy point process.

A key intermediate step in
[
15
]
toward the proof of
Theorem

1.1
is the following microscopic loop equation for the normalized adjacency matrix of a random
d
-regular graph perturbed by a small GOE. Its proof occupies the main part of
[
15
]
and is analogous to the derivation of the self-consistent equations (
4.3
). To make the error terms sufficiently small, we need to iterate the local resampling procedure many times and carefully track the accumulated errors.

Proposition 4.2
.

Fix a small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
. Let
\Delta_{t}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m_{N}(z;t)-m(z;t)
, and recall the microscopic window
\mathbf{M}
from (
4.7
). Fix
p\geq 1
and
w,w_{1},\ldots,w_{p-1}\in\mathbf{M}
, and set
z(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{t}+w
and
z_{j}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{t}+w_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq p-1
. Then for
N
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\Big(\Delta_{t}\!\big(z(t)\big)^{2}+2{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{z(t)-E_{t}}\;\Delta_{t}\!\big(z(t)\big)+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}\!\big(z(t);t\big)}{N}\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\Delta_{t}\!\big(z_{i}(t)\big)\right]\\
&\quad+\;\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq p-1}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{z}\left.\left(\frac{m_{N}\!\big(z;t\big)-m_{N}\!\big(z;t\big)}{z-z_{i}(t)}\right)\right|_{z=z_{i}(t)}\prod_{j:1\leqslant j\neq i\leqslant p-1}\Delta_{t}\!\big(z_{j}(t)\big)\right]=\OO\left(N^{-(p+1)/3-10{\mathfrak{c}}}\right).\end{split}

(4.12)

The scaling limit
N^{1/3}\Delta_{t}(E_{t}+w/({\mathcal{A}}N)^{2/3})/{\mathcal{A}}^{2/3}
converges to
S(w)-\sqrt{w}
in the microscopic version of loop equations (
4.11
). The error bound on the right-hand side of (
4.12
) is precisely calibrated so that, after scaling each factor of
\Delta_{t}
by
N^{1/3}
, the total error still vanishes as
N\to\infty
.

In what follows, we show that the microscopic loop equations (
4.12
) imply the Green’s function comparison in
Proposition

4.1
, thereby completing the final step of the three-step strategy. For clarity, we prove (
4.8
) only in the case
p=1
, writing
z(t)=E_{t}+w
with
w\in\bf M
. We refer to
[
15
, Section 3.3]
for the general case. In this case, (
4.8
) follows from integrating the following statement from
0
to
t=N^{-1/3+{\mathfrak{c}}}

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}\left[m_{N}(z(t);t)-m(z(t);t)\right]|\leqslant N^{-2{\mathfrak{c}}}.

(4.13)

The first loop equation corresponds to
p=1
in (
4.12
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\Delta_{t}(z(t))^{2}+2{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{z(t)-2}\Delta_{t}(z(t))+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z(t);t)}{N}\right]=\OO\left(N^{-2/3-10{\mathfrak{c}}}\right),

(4.14)

where
z(t)=E_{t}+w
with
w\in\bf M
. Next we show that (
4.14
) implies (
4.13
).
By Gaussian integration by parts,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\mathbb{E}\big[m_{N}(z;t)\big]\;=\;\frac{1}{N}\,\partial_{t}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\Tr\,(H+\sqrt{t}\,Z-z)^{-1}\right]\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{z}\!\left(m^{2}_{N}(z;t)+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z;t)}{N}\right)\right].

(4.15)

Subtracting the complex Burgers equation (
4.5
) for
s(z;t)
from (
4.15
) gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\mathbb{E}\big[m_{N}(z;t)-m(z;t)\big]\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\partial_{z}\!\left(m^{2}_{N}(z;t)-m^{2}(z;t)+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z;t)}{N}\right)\right].

(4.16)

Plugging
z(t)=E_{t}+w
with
w\in\mathbf{M}
into (
4.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\,\mathbb{E}\big[m_{N}(z(t);t)-m(z(t);t)\big]\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\partial_{z}F_{t}\big(z(t)\big)\big],\\
&F_{t}(z)\;:=\;\Delta_{t}(z)^{2}+2\big(m(z;t)-m(E_{t};t)\big)\Delta_{t}(z)+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z;t)}{N}.\end{split}

(4.17)

For
z
near the spectral edge, with
z-E_{t}\in\mathbf{M}
, and using the square-root behavior of
\varrho_{t}
, we have

m(z;t)-m(E_{t};t)\approx{\mathcal{A}}\,\sqrt{\,z-E_{t}\,}
.
Thus, neglecting the replacement error, the first loop equation (
4.14
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[F_{t}(z)\big]=\OO\left(N^{-2/3-10{\mathfrak{c}}}\right).

(4.18)

Since
F_{t}
is analytic on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
, Cauchy’s integral formula on a circle
\omega
centered at
z(t)
with radius
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)/2\geqslant N^{-2/3-{\mathfrak{c}}}/2
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\partial_{z}\left.F_{t}(z)\right|_{z=z(t)}\big]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[F_{t}(w)\big]}{(w-z(t))^{2}}{\rm d}w=\OO\left(N^{-8{\mathfrak{c}}}\right).

(4.19)

Combining (
4.17
) with (
4.19
) proves (
4.13
), and hence the Green’s function comparison
Proposition

4.1
.

4.3
Self-consistent equation and the first loop equation

To illustrate the main ideas, in these notes we present the proof of
the following
Theorem

4.5
, which yields the self-consistent equations and the first
microscopic version of the loop equations at the edge. We refer to

[
15
]
for the complete proof of edge universality.

We recall the functions
Y_{\ell},X_{\ell}
and the quantity
Q(z)
from
(
3.6
) and (
3.18
). For simplicity of notation, we write

\displaystyle Y(z)=Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z),\quad X(z)=X_{\ell}(Q(z),z).

(4.20)

We also recall the sets
\overline{\Omega}
and
\Omega
of
d
-regular
graphs from
Definition

3.1
and
Theorem

3.6
.

Next, we introduce some further error terms. We emphasize that they depend on
the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
and are therefore random quantities.

Definition 4.3
.

For any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
in the upper half–plane with
\eta=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z
, we introduce
the control parameter

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi(z)&:=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]}{N\eta}+\frac{1}{N^{1-2{\mathfrak{c}}}},\quad\Psi(z):=\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\left(\Phi(z)+\frac{|Q(z)-Y(z)|}{N^{{\mathfrak{b}}/8}}\right).\end{split}

(4.21)

Remark 4.4
.

The quantity
\Phi(z)
is a standard control parameter in random matrix
theory. We will control the expectation of
Q(z)-Y(z)
via
\Psi(z)
, see
(
4.22
). Although
\Psi(z)
itself contains
|Q(z)-Y(z)|
(but with an
extra factor
N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/8}
), we can (ignoring the absolute value) move this
term to the left-hand side and conclude that the expectation of
Q(z)-Y(z)
is
controlled by
\Phi(z)
.

The following result states that the expectation of the self-consistent
equations is negligible, and it gives the first loop equation corresponding to
the case
t=0
,
p=1
of
Proposition

4.2
. The proof of
Theorem

4.5

will be given in the remainder of this article.

Theorem 4.5
.

Fix a spectral parameter
z\in\bf D
(recall from (
1.8
)), and recall the control parameter

\Psi(z)
from (
4.21
). Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\bigl(Q(z)-Y(z)\bigr)\right]\lesssim(d-1)^{2\ell}\mathbb{E}[\Psi(z)],

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\bigl(m_{N}(z)-X(z)\bigr)\right]\lesssim(d-1)^{2\ell}\mathbb{E}[\Psi(z)].

(4.23)

Moreover, if we further assume that
|z-2|\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{g}}}
, then we have a
refined estimate for
Q(z)-Y(z)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\left(\frac{{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}{\ell+1}\bigl(Q(z)-Y(z)\bigr)+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z)}{N}\right)\right]=\OO\left(\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathbb{E}[\Psi(z)]}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}}\right),\end{split}

(4.24)

where the constant
{\mathcal{A}}=d(d-1)/(d-2)^{2}
is as in (
1.4
). If

|z+2|\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{g}}}
, an analogous statement holds after multiplying the first
term in (
4.24
) by
-1
.

Close to the right spectral edge, i.e. for

|z-2|\lesssim N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\gtrsim N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
, we
have

\displaystyle\begin{split}m_{\rm sc}(z)&=-1+\OO(\sqrt{|z-2|}),\quad 1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z)=2\sqrt{z-2}+\OO(|z-2|),\\
m_{d}(z)&=-\frac{d-1}{d-2}+\OO(\sqrt{|z-2|}),\end{split}

(4.25)

and the true sizes of the error terms are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]\lesssim\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{1/3}},\quad\Phi(z),\Psi(z)\lesssim\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{2/3}}.\end{split}

(4.26)

The optimal edge rigidity of eigenvalues in
[
15
]
follows
from establishing analogous high-moment estimates to (
4.22
) and
(
4.23
), which essentially lead to

\displaystyle|Q(z)-Y(z)|\lesssim\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{2/3}},\quad|m_{N}(z)-X(z)|\lesssim\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{1/2}}.

(4.27)

We remark that, for technical reasons, the estimate for
|m_{N}(z)-X(z)|
is
weaker. Nevertheless, these estimates are sufficient to derive optimal bounds
for
Q(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)
and
m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z)
, as explained below.

The concentration of
Q(z)
and
m_{N}(z)
can be derived from
(
4.27
). We also recall the expansion of
Y(z)=Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z)
from
(
3.7
) (by taking
\Delta=Q(z)
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}Q-Y=(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))-(Y-m_{\rm sc}(z))=(1-m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z))(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\\
&\quad-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)m_{d}(z)\left(\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{d-1}+\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell+2}(z)}{1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z)}\right)(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}+\OO\bigl(\ell^{5}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{3}\bigr)\\
&=(1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z))(\ell+1)(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+(\ell+1)(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}\\
&\quad+\OO\Bigl(\ell\bigl(|z-2||Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|+\sqrt{|z-2|}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\bigr)+\ell^{5}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{3}\Bigr)\\
&=(\ell+1)\Bigl(2\sqrt{z-2}\,(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+\OO\Bigl(\ell\bigl(|z-2||Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|+\sqrt{|z-2|}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\bigr)+\ell^{5}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{3}\Bigr),\end{split}

(4.28)

where we used (
4.25
) to replace
m_{\rm sc}(z),m_{d}(z),1-m_{\rm sc}^{2}(z)
by

-1,-(d-1)/(d-2),2\sqrt{z-2}
, respectively. Given that
Q-Y
is small as
in (
4.27
), we can analyze (
4.28
) as a quadratic equation in

Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)
with a small error. Such quadratic equations are ubiquitous in
random matrix theory; for example, for Wigner matrices. Using (
4.27
), the standard argument
shows that for
|z-2|\lesssim N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
, with high probability we have

\displaystyle|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|\lesssim\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{1/3}},

(4.29)

Using (
4.29
) we can simplify the error in (
4.28
), and get

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q-Y=(\ell+1)\Bigl(2\sqrt{z-2}\,(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))^{2}\Bigr)+\OO(N^{-1+\oo(1)}),\end{split}

(4.30)

Using the expansion of
X(z)=X_{\ell}(Q(z),z)
from (
3.8
) (with

\Delta=Q(z)
), we have

\displaystyle X-m_{d}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d-1}m_{d}^{2}(z)\,m_{\rm sc}^{2\ell}(z)\,(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO\bigl(|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO\bigl(\ell\sqrt{|z-2|}\,|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|+|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\bigr),

where we again used (
4.25
) to replace
m_{\rm sc}(z),m_{d}(z)
by

-1,-(d-1)/(d-2)
. It follows, by rearranging, that

\displaystyle\begin{split}m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z)&=m_{N}(z)-X+X-m_{d}(z)\\
&={\mathcal{A}}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO\bigl(|m_{N}(z)-X|+\ell\sqrt{|z-2|}|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|+|Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\bigr).\end{split}

(4.31)

Combining (
4.27
), (
4.29
) and (
4.31
), we conclude
that for
|z-2|\lesssim N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
, with high probability

\displaystyle m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z)={\mathcal{A}}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\OO\left(\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{1/2}}\right)=\OO\left(\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{1/3}}\right).

(4.32)

We remark that (
4.24
) is slightly different from the loop
equation (
4.12
) (with
t=0
and
p=1
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[(m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z))^{2}+2{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{z-2}\,(m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z))+\frac{\partial_{z}m_{N}(z)}{N}\right]=\OO\left(N^{-2/3-10{\mathfrak{c}}}\right).\end{split}

(4.33)

By plugging (
4.32
) into (
4.30
), we conclude that for

|z-2|\lesssim N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
, with high probability

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(Q-Y)}{\ell+1}=(m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z))^{2}+2{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{z-2}\,(m_{N}(z)-m_{d}(z))+\OO\left(\frac{N^{\oo(1)}}{N^{5/6}}\right).

(4.34)

The claim (
4.33
) then follows from plugging (
4.34
) into (
4.24
), and
the error term comes from

\displaystyle\frac{N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathbb{E}[\Psi(z)]}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}}\lesssim\frac{N^{{\mathfrak{o}}+\oo(1)}}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}N^{2/3}}\lesssim N^{-2/3-10{\mathfrak{c}}},

where we used (
4.26
).

5
Local resampling

In this section, we recall the local resampling and its properties. This gives us the framework to talk about resampling from the random regular graph distribution as a way to get an improvement in our estimates of the Green’s function.

For any graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we denote the set of unoriented edges by
E({\mathcal{G}})
,
and the set of oriented edges by
\vec{E}({\mathcal{G}}):=\{(u,v),(v,u):\{u,v\}\in E({\mathcal{G}})\}
.
For a subset
\vec{S}\subset\vec{E}({\mathcal{G}})
, we denote by
S
the set of corresponding non-oriented edges.
For a subset
S\subset E({\mathcal{G}})
of edges we denote by
[S]\subset[\![{N}]\!]
the set of vertices incident to any edge in
S
.
Moreover, for a subset
\mathbb{V}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
of vertices, we define
E({\mathcal{G}})|_{\mathbb{V}}
to be the subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}
induced by
\mathbb{V}
.

\mathcal{G}
o
\mathcal{T}
i
l_{\mu}
l_{1}=l_{2}
l_{3}=l_{4}
a_{\mu}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
b_{\mu}
c_{\mu}
b_{1}
c_{1}
b_{2}
c_{2}
b_{3}
c_{3}
b_{4}
c_{4}

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
o
\mathcal{T}
i
l_{\mu}
l_{1}=l_{2}
l_{3}=l_{4}
b_{\mu}
c_{\mu}
b_{1}
c_{1}
b_{2}
c_{2}
b_{3}
c_{3}
b_{4}
c_{4}
a_{\mu}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}

Figure 3
:

An example of the local resampling performed on the graph is as follows. We replace the blue edges, located on the boundary of the radius-
\ell
neighborhood of a vertex
o
, with randomly chosen edges from the graph. Together, these edges constitute the resampling data, denoted by
\mathbf{S}
. This operation creates new red edges and establishes a new boundary.

Definition 5.1
.

A (simple) switching is encoded by two oriented edges
\vec{S}=\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{4})\}\subset\vec{E}
.
We assume that the two edges are disjoint, i.e. that
|\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}|=4
.
Then the switching consists of
replacing the edges
\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{3},v_{4}\}
with the edges
\{v_{1},v_{4}\},\{v_{2},v_{3}\}
.
We denote the graph after the switching
\vec{S}
by
T_{\vec{S}}({\mathcal{G}})
,
and the new edges
\vec{S}^{\prime}=\{(v_{1},v_{4}),(v_{2},v_{3})\}
by

T(\vec{S})=\vec{S}^{\prime}
.

The local resampling involves a fixed center vertex, which we now assume to be vertex
o
,
and a radius
\ell
.
Given a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we write
{\mathcal{T}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
to denote the radius-
\ell
neighborhood of
o
(which may not necessarily be a tree) and write
\mathbb{T}
for its vertex set.
The edge boundary
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}
of
{\mathcal{T}}
consists of the edges in
{\mathcal{G}}
with one vertex in
\mathbb{T}
and the other vertex in
[\![{N}]\!]\setminus\mathbb{T}
.
We enumerate the edges of
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}
as
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\mu}\}
, where
e_{\alpha}=\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}\}
with
l_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{T}
and
a_{\alpha}\in[\![{N}]\!]\setminus\mathbb{T}
. We orient the edges
e_{\alpha}
by defining
\vec{e}_{\alpha}=(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha})
.
We notice that
\mu
and the edges
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\mu}
depend on
{\mathcal{G}}
. The edges
e_{\alpha}
are distinct, but
the vertices
a_{\alpha}
are not necessarily distinct and neither are the vertices
l_{\alpha}
. Our local resampling switches the edge boundary of
{\mathcal{T}}
with randomly chosen edges in
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}

if the switching is admissible (see below), and leaves them in place otherwise.
To perform our local resampling, see
Figure

3
, we choose
(b_{1},c_{1}),\dots,(b_{\mu},c_{\mu})
to be independent, uniformly chosen oriented edges from the graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
, i.e.,
the oriented edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
that are not incident to
\mathbb{T}
,
and define

\vec{S}_{\alpha}=\{\vec{e}_{\alpha},(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\},\qquad{\bf S}=(\vec{S}_{1},\vec{S}_{2},\dots,\vec{S}_{\mu}).

(5.1)

The sets
\bf S
will be called the
resampling data
for
{\mathcal{G}}
. We remark that repetitions are allowed in the data
(b_{1},c_{1}),(b_{2},c_{2}),\cdots,(b_{\mu},c_{\mu})
.
We define an indicator that will be crucial to the definition of the switch.

Definition 5.2
.

For
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
we define the indicator functions

I_{\alpha}\equiv I_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}},{\bf S})=1
if

1.

the subgraph
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})})
after adding the edge
\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\}
is a tree;

2.

and
\dist_{{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},\{a_{\beta},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})>{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}
for all
\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\setminus\{\alpha\}
.

The indicator function
I_{\alpha}
imposes two conditions. The first one is a “tree” condition, which ensures that

a_{\alpha}
and
\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}
are far away from each other, and their neighborhoods are trees.
The second one imposes an “isolation” condition, which ensures that we only perform simple switching when the switching pair is far away from other switching pairs. In this way, we do not need to keep track of the interaction between different simple switchings.

We define the
admissible set

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}:=\{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]:I_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}},{\bf S})\}.

(5.2)

We say that the index
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
is
switchable
if
\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}
. We denote the set
\mathbb{W}_{\bf S}=\{b_{\alpha}:\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}\}
. Let
\nu:=|{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}|
be the number of admissible switchings and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\nu}

be an arbitrary enumeration of
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}
.
Then we define the switched graph by

T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}):=\left(T_{\vec{S}_{\alpha_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{\vec{S}_{\alpha_{\nu}}}\right)({\mathcal{G}}),

(5.3)

and the resampling data by

T({\bf S}):=(T_{1}(\vec{S}_{1}),\dots,T_{\mu}(\vec{S}_{\mu})),\quad T_{\alpha}(\vec{S}_{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{cases}T(\vec{S}_{\alpha})&(\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}),\\
\vec{S}_{\alpha}&(\alpha\not\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}).\end{cases}

(5.4)

To make the structure more clear, we introduce an enlarged probability space.
Equivalent to the definition above, the sets
\vec{S}_{\alpha}
as defined in (
5.1
) are uniformly distributed over

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}})=\{\vec{S}\subset\vec{E}:\vec{S}=\{\vec{e}_{\alpha},\vec{e}\},\text{$\vec{e}$ is not incident to ${\mathcal{T}}$}\},

i.e., the set of pairs of oriented edges in
\vec{E}
containing
\vec{e}_{\alpha}
and another oriented edge in
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
.
Therefore
{\bf S}=(\vec{S}_{1},\vec{S}_{2},\dots,\vec{S}_{\mu})
is uniformly distributed over the set

{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})=\sf S_{1}({\mathcal{G}})\times\cdots\times\sf S_{\mu}({\mathcal{G}})
.

We introduce the following notation on the probability and expectation with respect to the randomness of the
{\bf S}\in\sf S({\mathcal{G}})
.

Definition 5.3
.

Given any
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we
denote
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}(\cdot)
the uniform probability measure on
{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
;
and
\mathbb{E}_{\bf S}[\cdot]
the expectation over the choice of
{\bf S}
according to
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}
.

The following claim from
[
17
, Lemma 7.3]
states that this switch is invariant under the random regular graph distribution.

Lemma 5.4

(
[
17
, Lemma 7.3]
)
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
. We recall the operator
T_{\bf S}
from (
5.3
). Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a random
d
-regular graph and
{\bf S}
uniformly distributed over
{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
, then the graph pair
({\mathcal{G}},T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}))
forms an exchangeable pair:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{G}},T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}(T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}),{\mathcal{G}}).

5.1
Green’s function of resampled graph

Fix an edge
\{i,o\}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
, we recall the resampling data
{\bf S}=\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
around
o
from
Section

5
, denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}{\mathcal{G}}
. In the remainder of the paper, we denote by
\widetilde{H}
the normalized adjacency matrix of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
. Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}-H=-\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha},\quad\xi_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d-1}}\left(\Delta_{l_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}}+\Delta_{b_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{l_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{a_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}\right).

(5.5)

Its Green’s function and the Stieltjes transform of its empirical eigenvalue distribution are denoted as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(z)=(\widetilde{H}-z)^{-1},\quad\widetilde{m}_{N}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\Tr\widetilde{G}(z).

(5.6)

In the rest of this section we collect some basic estimates of the Green’s functions of
\widetilde{G}(z)
. Their proofs follow from analyzing Green’s functions using the resolvent identity formula (
2.1
).

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
satisfy
|z|\leqslant 1/{\mathfrak{g}}
, and set
\eta:=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Fix a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
(recall
Definition

3.7
) and vertices

\{i,o\}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
with vertex set
\mathbb{T}
.
We denote the resampling data
{\bf S}=\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
around
o
(recall from
Section

5
), and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}{\mathcal{G}}
. Moreover, we also assume that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
.

Then, for any
{\mathbb{X}}
of the form

{\mathbb{X}}\in\{\emptyset,\mathbb{T}\}
and any
x,y\not\in{\mathbb{X}}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\widetilde{G}^{({\mathbb{X}})}_{xy}(z)-P^{({\mathbb{X}})}_{xy}\bigl({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/100}(\{x,y\}\cup{\mathbb{X}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{\rm sc}(z)\bigr)\bigr|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}.

(5.7)

Moreover, the following holds

\displaystyle|\widetilde{m}_{N}(z)-m_{N}(z)|\lesssim\frac{(d-1)^{\ell}N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]}{N\eta},

(5.8)

Proof.

The first statement (
5.7
) follows from our assumption that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
and (
3.26
) (with
{\mathbb{X}}=\mathbb{T}
).

For the second statement, we recall the notations from (
5.5
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}|\widetilde{m}_{N}(z)-m_{N}(z)|=\frac{1}{N}\Tr(\widetilde{H}-z)^{-1}-\frac{1}{N}\Tr(H-z)^{-1}=\frac{1}{N}\Tr(\widetilde{H}-z)^{-1}(H-\widetilde{H})(H-z)^{-1}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\Tr(\widetilde{H}-z)^{-1}\xi_{\alpha}(H-z)^{-1}\\
&\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{d-1}N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}(|\widetilde{G}_{il_{\alpha}}|+|\widetilde{G}_{ia_{\alpha}}|+|\widetilde{G}_{ib_{\alpha}}|+|\widetilde{G}_{ic_{\alpha}}|)(|G_{a_{\alpha}i}|+|G_{b_{\alpha}i}|+|G_{c_{\alpha}i}|+|G_{l_{\alpha}i}|)\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d-1}N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}(|\widetilde{G}_{il_{\alpha}}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{ia_{\alpha}}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{ib_{\alpha}}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{ic_{\alpha}}|^{2}+|G_{a_{\alpha}i}|^{2}+|G_{b_{\alpha}i}|^{2}+|G_{c_{\alpha}i}|^{2}+|G_{l_{\alpha}i}|^{2})\\
&\lesssim\frac{(d-1)^{\ell}N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\widetilde{m}_{N}(z)]}{N\eta}+\frac{(d-1)^{\ell}N^{\mathfrak{o}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{N}(z)]}{N\eta}\end{split}

(5.9)

where the first two statements is from resolvent identity (
2.1
); the third and fourth statements follows from (
5.5
); the fifth statement follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality; the sixth statement follows from the Ward identity (
2.6
), (
3.22
) and
\mu=\OO((d-1)^{\ell})
.
Since
N\eta\geqslant N^{\mathfrak{g}}\gg(d-1)^{\ell}N^{\mathfrak{o}}
, the claim (
5.8
) follows from rearranging (
5.9
).

∎

6
Concentration via exchangeable pair

In this section, we explain the basic ideas of proving concentration using
exchangeable pairs, and discuss how to apply this approach in the setting of
random
d
-regular graphs.

6.1
Sum of independent Bernoulli random variables

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
be independent
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{i})
random variables and define

W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},\qquad\mu=\mathbb{E}W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}.

In this section, we explain how to use exchangeable pair to show that
W
concentrates.

Choose
I\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{1,\dots,n\}
independent of everything and let

X_{I},X_{I}^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{I})
be independent copies.
Set

W^{\prime}=W-X_{I}+X_{I}^{\prime}.

Then
(W,W^{\prime})
is an exchangeable pair and satisfies
|W^{\prime}-W|\leq 1
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W^{\prime}-W\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(p_{i}-X_{i})=-\frac{1}{n}(W-\mu)=:-f(W).

(6.1)

For any smooth
g
,

\mathbb{E}[f(W)g(W)]=\mathbb{E}[(W-W^{\prime})g(W)]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\!\big[(W-W^{\prime})(g(W)-g(W^{\prime}))\big].

Take
g(w)=f^{2p-1}(w)
with
f(w)=(w-\mu)/n
from (
6.1
). Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[f^{2p}(W)]&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\!\big[(W-W^{\prime})(f^{2p-1}(W)-f^{2p-1}(W^{\prime}))\big]\\
&\leqslant\frac{2p-1}{4}\mathbb{E}\!\big[(W-W^{\prime})(f(W)-f(W^{\prime})(f^{2p-2}(W)+f^{2p-2}(W^{\prime}))\big]\\
&=\frac{2p-1}{2n}\mathbb{E}\!\big[(W-W^{\prime})^{2}f^{2p-2}(W)\big]=\frac{2p-1}{2n}\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}\!\big[(W-W^{\prime})^{2}|W]f^{2p-2}(W)\big].\end{split}

(6.2)

We can then bound the inner expectation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(W-W^{\prime})^{2}\mid W]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(X_{i}-X_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\mid W]\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(X_{i}+X_{i}^{\prime})\mid W]=\frac{W+\mu}{n}.

(6.3)

By plugging (
6.3
) into (
6.2
), we arrive at the following moment bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[f^{2p}(W)]\leqslant\frac{2p-1}{2n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(W+\mu)f^{2p-2}(W)\big]=\frac{2p-1}{2n}\mathbb{E}[f^{2p-1}(W)\big]+\frac{(2p-1)\mu}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[f^{2p-2}(W)\big].\end{split}

(6.4)

We can upper bound the odd moments by even moments using Hölder’s inequality. Then recursively, we can bound the
2p
-th moment as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sum_{i}(X_{i}-p_{i})}{n}\right)^{2p}\right]=\mathbb{E}[f^{2p}(W)]\leqslant\frac{C_{p}\mu^{p}}{n^{2p}}

6.2
Random d-regular graphs

In our setting, we choose a directed edge
(i,o)
uniformly at random, independently of everything else, and let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}
be the graph obtained by locally resampling the neighborhood of the vertex
o
. Then
({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
form an exchangeable pair. In next section we will show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}\,\big|\,{\mathcal{G}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{S}}\!\left[\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}\right]=Y+{\mathcal{E}}_{(i,o)}({\mathcal{G}}),

where we recall
Y
from (
4.20
), and
{\mathcal{E}}_{(i,o)}({\mathcal{G}})
is small (here
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{S}}
denotes expectation over the resampling randomness). This implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\widetilde{Q}-Q\,\big|\,{\mathcal{G}}\right]=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim o}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{S}}\!\left[\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}\right]-Q=Y-Q+{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{G}}),\qquad{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{G}}):=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim o}{\mathcal{E}}_{(i,o)}({\mathcal{G}}).

An argument analogous to the exchangeable-pairs proof of concentration for sums of Bernoulli random variables yields high-moment bounds for
\widetilde{Q}-Q
, and hence concentration of
\widetilde{Q}-Q
. Although the individual errors
{\mathcal{E}}_{(i,o)}({\mathcal{G}})
are small—by (
3.21
) and
Lemma

3.9
one obtains bounds of order
\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}})
—these are not sufficient to obtain the optimal eigenvalue concentration. For example in
Theorem

4.5
, near the spectral edge
|z\pm 2|=N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
, the optimal error is
N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
. To attain this optimal rate, we perform additional local resampling steps to capture and control the fluctuations of the error terms
{\mathcal{E}}_{(i,o)}({\mathcal{G}})
more precisely. This is outlined in
Section

8
and
Section

11
.

7
Expectation of
Q(z)
.

In this section we take the first step computing the expectation of
Q(z)-Y(z)
.
