\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(Q-Y)\right]&=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{o,i}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{oi}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right].\end{split}

(7.1)

For
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
from
Definition

3.1
, the number of vertices that do not have a tree neighborhood of radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
is at most
N^{\mathfrak{c}}
. We can restrict the righthand side of (
7.1
) to the sum over vertices
o
which has radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{o,i}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{oi}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right]=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{o,i}\mathbb{E}\left[I(\{o,i\},{\mathcal{G}}){\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right]+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-{\mathfrak{c}}}}\right),\end{split}

(7.2)

where

\displaystyle I(\{o,i\},{\mathcal{G}})=A_{oi}\bm{1}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is a tree}).

(7.3)

To compute the righthand side of (
7.1
), condition on
I(\{o,i\},{\mathcal{G}})=1
we perform a local resampling around
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{F}}
using the resampling data
{\bf S}=\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, where
\mu:=d(d-1)^{\ell}
is the number of boundary edges of the
d
-regular tree truncated at depth
\ell+1
. We denote the new graph as
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}})
, with its corresponding Green’s function
\widetilde{G}
.

\mathcal{G}
o
\mathcal{T}
i
l_{\mu}
l_{1}=l_{2}
l_{3}=l_{4}

Figure 4
:
We view
{\mathcal{F}}
(corresponding to the solid edges) as an embedded subgraph of

{\mathcal{G}}
, which contains all the switching edges. The indicator

I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
signifies that
o,c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\mu}
have tree
neighborhoods and are well separated.

We introduce the following subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}

{\mathcal{F}}=B_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\cup\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}=B_{\ell+1}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\cup\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}=({\bf i},E),

(7.4)

which contains all the switching edges, see
Figure

4
. To ensure switching edges are well separated and lie in large tree neighborhoods, we
use the following indicator.

\displaystyle I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}):=\prod_{\{x,y\}\in E}A_{xy}\;\prod_{c\in\{o,c_{1},\cdots,c_{\mu}\}}\;\prod_{x\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c;{\mathcal{G}})}\bm{1}\!\big({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(x;{\mathcal{G}})\ \text{is a tree}\big)\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\neq c^{\prime}\in\{o,c_{1},\cdots,c_{\mu}\}\end{subarray}}\bm{1}\!\big(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(c,c^{\prime})\geq 3{\mathfrak{R}}\big).

(7.5)

Condition on
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
. Ignoring vertex labels, the graph
{\mathcal{F}}
is simply
a forest consisting of a
d
-regular tree truncated at depth
\ell+1
together
with
\mu
disjoint edges. Thus the averaging over the edge
(i,o)
in
(
7.1
), together with the randomness in the resampling data

\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, can be viewed as an average
over embeddings of
{\mathcal{F}}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
.

To formalize this, let
{\mathcal{F}}=(V,E)
denote the
template
whose vertices

V
are formal symbols, consisting of the truncated
d
-regular tree of depth

\ell+1
and the
\mu
disjoint edges. The vertices become concrete vertices
of
{\mathcal{G}}
only after an embedding is chosen. An embedding is specified by an
assignment
{\bf i}\in[\![{N}]\!]^{|V|}
; different choices of
{\bf i}
yield
different embeddings. Once an embedding
{\bf i}
is fixed, we write

{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
, and the indicator
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
in (
7.5
) is
well defined.

Claim 7.1
.

We perform a local resampling around vertex
o
, and denote the resampled graph by
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(Q-Y)\right]&=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{o,i}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{oi}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)(\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right]+\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]),\end{split}

(7.6)

where the normalization constant
Z_{\mathcal{F}}
is not random, and for any
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
, it satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-2{\mathfrak{c}}}}\right)\right).

(7.7)

The relation (
7.7
) follows from the fact that most vertices of
{\mathcal{G}}
has large tree neighborhood, so the number of “good” embeddings of
{\mathcal{F}}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
concentrates.

If we temporarily ignore the indicator and the averaging over embeddings, the
claim reduces to the simple symmetry

\mathbb{E}\!\left[A_{oi}\,(G^{(i)}_{oo}-Y)\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[A_{oi}\,(\widetilde{G}^{(i)}_{oo}-Y)\right],

which follows from that
({\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}})
are identically distributed under
the local resampling. The indicator
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
just restrict attention to “good” placements where we have good estimates for the Green’s function. We omit the proof of
7.1
.

In the following proposition, we show that the expectation (
7.6
) breaks down into an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the same form, in the following sense.

Definition 7.2
.

We say that
\mathcal{U}
is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-weighted sum of elements of
\mathcal{R}
if there exist finitely many terms
R_{1},\cdots,R_{m}\in\mathcal{R}
(with
m
possibly depending on
N
) and coefficients
{\mathfrak{c}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathfrak{c}}_{m}
such that

\mathcal{U}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\mathfrak{c}}_{j}\,R_{j},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{j=1}^{m}|{\mathfrak{c}}_{j}|=\mathcal{O}(1).

Proposition 7.3
.

We recall
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
from (
7.4
), and view it as embedded in
{\mathcal{G}}
with vertices given by
{\bf i}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)(\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y)\right]=I_{1}+I_{2}+{\mathcal{E}},

(7.8)

where
|{\mathcal{E}}|=\OO((d-1)^{2\ell}\mathbb{E}[\Psi])
, and

1.

I_{1}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms of the following form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d-1)^{{\mathfrak{q}}\ell/2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)],

(7.9)

where
{\mathfrak{q}}\geqslant 0
;

2.

I_{2}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms of the following form

\displaystyle\frac{(d-1)^{3r\ell}}{(d-1)^{{\mathfrak{q}}\ell/2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)R_{{\bf i}}].

(7.10)

Here,
{\mathfrak{q}}\geqslant 0
and
r\geqslant 2
, and the function
R_{{\bf i}}
contains
r
factors of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\{(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},\quad\{G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})},G_{b_{\alpha}b_{\beta}}\}_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},\quad(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)).\end{split}

(7.11)

7.1
Switching using
the Schur complement formula

To prove
Proposition

7.3
, we need to rewrite
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y
which depends on the Green’s functions of the switched graph
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
, in terms of the Green’s functions of the original graph

{\mathcal{G}}
, see
Figure

3
. This is achieved as follows.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
and
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
from (
7.5
), and define the index set
{\mathsf{A}}_{i}:=\{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]:\dist_{{\mathcal{T}}}(i,l_{\alpha})=\ell+1\}
, see
Figure

5
. The following holds:

\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y
can be rewritten as a weighted sum

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y&=\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)^{2\ell+2}}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}(G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q)+\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)^{2\ell+2}}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}+{\mathcal{U}}+{\mathcal{E}}.\end{split}

(7.12)

where

{\mathcal{U}}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the form
(d-1)^{3(r-1)\ell}R_{r}
, where
r\geqslant 2
, and
R_{r}
contains
r
factors of the form

\displaystyle(G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q),\quad G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})},\quad Q-m_{\rm sc}(z),\quad\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!],

(7.13)

and at least one of them is
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
or
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
; and the error
{\mathcal{E}}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}&=\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}})+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{{\mathfrak{b}}/2}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\\
&=\OO\left(\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(7.14)

\mathcal{G}
o
\mathcal{T}
i
l_{\mu}
l_{1}=l_{2}
l_{3}=l_{4}
l_{2\nu-1}=l_{2\nu}
a_{\mu}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}

Figure 5
:
The index set
{\mathsf{A}}_{i}:=\{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]:\dist_{{\mathcal{T}}}(i,l_{\alpha})=\ell+1\}
is given by
\{1,2,\cdots,2\nu\}
, corresponding to these red nodes.

Remark 7.5
.

The expansion (
7.12
) decomposes
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y
into two leading contributions and a higher–order remainder
{\mathcal{U}}
, which together comprise weighted sums of terms built from the factors in (
7.13
).
By
Lemma

3.9
, each factor in (
7.13
) is bounded by
N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
with high probability. Consequently, any product
R_{r}
of
r
such factors satisfies
|R_{r}|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}r}
with high probability. The term
{\mathcal{U}}
collects an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of contributions of the form
(d-1)^{3(r-1)\ell}R_{r}
with
r\geqslant 2
, so that

(d-1)^{3(r-1)\ell}\,|R_{r}|\lesssim(d-1)^{3(r-1)\ell}N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}r}.

Therefore, every contribution to
{\mathcal{U}}
is higher–order (by at least one extra factor
N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
) compared to the two leading sums in (
7.12
).

Remark 7.6
.

More explicitly, the term
{\mathcal{U}}
in (
7.12
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{U}}=\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+2}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}L^{(i)}_{l_{\alpha}l_{\alpha}}(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)^{2}+\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+2}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i},\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\atop\alpha\neq\beta}(L^{(i)}_{l_{\beta}l_{\beta}}+L^{(i)}_{l_{\alpha}l_{\beta}})(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})})^{2}\\
&+\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)(G_{c_{\beta}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\beta})}-Q)+\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\atop\alpha^{\prime}\neq\beta^{\prime}\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{2}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)G_{c_{\alpha^{\prime}}c_{\beta^{\prime}}}^{(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}b_{\beta^{\prime}})}\\
&+\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!],\alpha^{\prime}\neq\beta^{\prime}\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\atop\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\}}{\mathfrak{c}}_{3}(\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}G_{c_{\alpha^{\prime}}c_{\beta^{\prime}}}^{(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}b_{\beta^{\prime}})}+\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{4}(\alpha)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\\
&+\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{5}(\alpha,\beta)G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))+\textnormal{terms of the form }\{(d-1)^{3(r-1)\ell}R_{r}\}_{r\geqslant 3},\end{split}

(7.15)

where the total sum of the coefficients
{\mathfrak{c}}_{1},{\mathfrak{c}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathfrak{c}}_{5}
is bounded by
\OO(1)
, and

\displaystyle L^{(i)}=P^{(i)}({\mathcal{T}},z,m_{\rm sc}(z))=\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-m_{\rm sc}(z)\mathbb{I}^{\partial}},\quad\mathbb{I}^{\partial}_{xy}=\bm{1}(\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,o)=\ell)\delta_{xy},\text{ for }x,y\in\mathbb{T},

(7.16)

which is the Green’s function of the
(d-1)
-ary tree.

In the rest we prove
Lemma

7.4
. We start with the Schur complement formulas which will be used to prove
Lemma

7.4
. Let
\widetilde{B}
be the normalized adjacency matrix of the directed edges
\{(c_{\alpha},l_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
. Then the adjacency matrices
\widetilde{H}^{(i)}
is in the block form

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{(i)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}H^{(i)}_{\mathbb{T}}&\widetilde{B}^{\top}\\
\widetilde{B}&\widetilde{H}_{\mathbb{T}^{\complement}}\end{array}\right].

We also denote the Green’s function of
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
as
G^{(\mathbb{T})}
and
\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}
respectively.

We collect some estimates below, which will be used later. Recall from
Proposition

3.3
, for
x,y\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}
,
|L_{xy}^{(i)}|\lesssim(d-1)^{-\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,y)/2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|L^{(i)}_{ox}|\lesssim\sum_{r=0}^{\ell}(d-1)^{r/2}\lesssim(d-1)^{\ell/2},\\
&\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|L^{(i)}_{xy}|\lesssim\sum_{r=0}^{\ell}(d-1)^{\ell-r}\left(\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r}(d-1)^{r^{\prime}/2}+\sum_{r^{\prime}=r+1}^{2\ell-r}(d-1)^{r/2}\right)\lesssim\ell(d-1)^{\ell},\end{split}

(7.17)

where for the first sum, we used that
|\{x\in\mathbb{T}:\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(o,x)=r\}|=\OO((d-1)^{r})
for
0\leqslant r\leqslant\ell
. For the second sum, we consider
\{x\in\mathbb{T}:\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(o,x)=\ell-r\}
. Note that there are
\OO((d-1)^{\ell-r})
such vertices. Given such
x
, for any
y\in\mathbb{T}
,
0\leqslant\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,y)\leqslant 2\ell-r
.
There are two cases depending on
\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,y)=r^{\prime}
. If
0\leqslant r^{\prime}\leqslant r
, we have
|\{y\in\mathbb{T}:\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,y)=r^{\prime}\}|=\OO((d-1)^{r^{\prime}})
, and if
r+1\leqslant r^{\prime}\leqslant 2\ell-r
we have
|\{y\in\mathbb{T}:\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(x,y)=r^{\prime}\}|=\OO((d-1)^{(r+r^{\prime})/2})
. The second statement in (
7.17
) follows from summing over
r,r^{\prime}
and using
|L_{xy}^{(i)}|\lesssim(d-1)^{-r^{\prime}/2}
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.4
.

Thanks to the Schur complement formula (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-{\widetilde{B}}^{\top}\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}{\widetilde{B}}}\right)_{oo}.

Since
o
has a radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood,
H^{(i)}_{\mathbb{T}}
is the normalized adjacency matrix of a truncated
(d-1)
-ary tree, and
L^{(i)}
from (
7.16
) agrees with the Green’s function of
(d-1)
-ary tree (see (
3.3
)),

\displaystyle m_{\rm sc}(z)=L^{(i)}_{oo}=P_{oo}^{(i)}({\mathcal{T}},z,m_{\rm sc}(z))=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-m_{\rm sc}(z)\mathbb{I}^{\partial}}\right)_{oo}=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}m_{\rm sc}(z)\widetilde{B}}\right)_{oo},

By taking the difference of the two above expressions, we have

\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-m_{\rm sc}(z)=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}m_{\rm sc}(z)\widetilde{B}-{\mathcal{D}}}-\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}m_{\rm sc}(z)\widetilde{B}}\right)_{oo},

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{D}}=\widetilde{B}^{\top}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\widetilde{B}+{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\quad{\mathcal{D}}_{1}=\widetilde{B}^{\top}(\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}-Q)\widetilde{B},\end{split}

(7.18)

are matrices indexed by
(\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\})\times(\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\})
.

By our assumption
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
. Thanks to (
3.21
) and
Lemma

3.9
, we have

\displaystyle|({\mathcal{D}}_{1})_{xy}|,|{\mathcal{D}}_{xy}|\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}},\text{ for }x,y\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}.

Thus, for some sufficiently large constant
{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-m_{\rm sc}(z)&=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}m_{\rm sc}(z)\widetilde{B}-{\mathcal{D}}}-\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}m_{\rm sc}(z)\widetilde{B}}\right)_{oo}\\
&=\left(L^{(i)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathfrak{p}}\left({\mathcal{D}}L^{(i)}\right)^{k}\right)_{oo}+\OO(N^{-2}).\end{split}

(7.19)

Recall
Y=Y_{\ell}(Q,z)
from (
3.6
) and (
4.20
):

\displaystyle Y=\left(\frac{1}{H_{\mathbb{T}}^{(i)}-z-\widetilde{B}^{\top}Q\widetilde{B}}\right)_{oo}.

By the same argument as in (
7.19
) we also have that

\displaystyle Y-m_{\rm sc}(z)=\left(L^{(i)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathfrak{p}}\left(\widetilde{B}^{\top}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\widetilde{B}L^{(i)}\right)^{k}\right)_{oo}+\OO(N^{-2}).

(7.20)

By taking the difference of (
7.19
) and (
7.20
), up to error
\OO(N^{-2})
, we get that the difference
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y
is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}(L^{(i)}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}L^{(i)})_{oo}&+\left(L^{(i)}\sum_{k=2}^{\mathfrak{p}}\left({\mathcal{D}}L^{(i)}\right)^{k}\right)_{oo}\\
&-\left(L^{(i)}\sum_{k=2}^{\mathfrak{p}}\left((\widetilde{B}^{\top}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\widetilde{B}L^{(i)}\right)^{k}\right)_{oo}.\end{split}

(7.21)

If
\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}
, then
\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(i,l_{\alpha})=\ell+1
, and
Proposition

3.3
gives

\displaystyle L^{(i)}_{ol_{\alpha}}=m_{\rm sc}(z)\left(-\frac{{m_{\rm sc}(z)}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(o,l_{\alpha})},\quad|L^{(i)}_{ol_{\alpha}}|\lesssim(d-1)^{-\dist_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,l_{\alpha})/2}=(d-1)^{-\ell/2}.

(7.22)

Otherwise if
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\setminus{\mathsf{A}}_{i}
, then
o,l_{\alpha}
are in different connected components of
{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
, and
|L^{(i)}_{ol_{\alpha}}|=0
.
Thus the first term in (
7.21
) can be computed as,

\displaystyle(L^{(i)}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}L^{(i)})_{oo}=\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)^{2\ell+2}}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q)+\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)^{2\ell+2}}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}},

(7.23)

We obtain the first two terms in (
7.12
), after replacing
\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-Q,\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
in (
7.23
) by
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})},G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
. We collect the difference in the error term
{\mathcal{E}}
(as in (
7.14
)).

For the terms
k\geqslant 2
, in general for any matrix
V
defined on
(\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\})\times(\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\})
,

L^{(i)}(VL^{(i)})^{k}
is given as a sum of terms in the following form

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}L^{(i)}_{ox_{1}}V_{x_{1}x_{2}}L^{(i)}_{x_{2}x_{3}}V_{x_{3}x_{4}}L^{(i)}_{x_{4}x_{5}}\cdots V_{x_{2k-1}x_{2k}}L^{(i)}_{x_{2k}o}.

(7.24)

We can reorganize (
7.24
) in the following way

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}L^{(i)}_{ox_{1}}L^{(i)}_{x_{2}x_{3}}\cdots L^{(i)}_{x_{2k}o}V_{x_{1}x_{2}}V_{x_{3}x_{4}}\cdots V_{x_{2k-1}x_{2k}}.\\
&=(d-1)^{3(k-1)\ell}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}(d-1)^{-3(k-1)\ell}L^{(i)}_{ox_{1}}L^{(i)}_{x_{2}x_{3}}\cdots L^{(i)}_{x_{2k}o}V_{x_{1}x_{2}}V_{x_{3}x_{4}}\cdots V_{x_{2k-1}x_{2k}}\\
&=:(d-1)^{3(k-1)\ell}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}{\mathfrak{c}}_{{\bm{x}}}V_{x_{1}x_{2}}V_{x_{3}x_{4}}\cdots V_{x_{2k-1}x_{2k}},\end{split}

(7.25)

where the weights
{\mathfrak{c}}_{{\bm{x}}}=(d-1)^{-3(k-1)\ell}L^{(i)}_{ox_{1}}\cdots L^{(i)}_{x_{2k}o}
, and the total weights are bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|{\mathfrak{c}}_{{\bm{x}}}|

\displaystyle=(d-1)^{-3(k-1)\ell}\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|L^{(i)}_{ox_{1}}|\sum_{x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|L^{(i)}_{x_{2}x_{3}}|\cdots\sum_{x_{2k}\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{i\}}|L^{(i)}_{x_{2k}o}|

\displaystyle\lesssim(d-1)^{-3(k-1)\ell}\ell^{k-1}(d-1)^{k\ell}=(d-1)^{-(2k-3)\ell}\ell^{k-1}\lesssim 1,

where to get the second line we used (
7.17
); in the last inequality, we used that
k\geqslant 2
.

To compute the difference for
k\geqslant 2
in (
7.21
), we consider two possible forms for
V
:

V=\widetilde{B}^{\top}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\widetilde{B}
or
V=\widetilde{B}^{\top}(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z))\widetilde{B}+{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
. As discussed above (see (
7.25
)), terms in (
7.21
) with
k\geqslant 2
break down to an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the form
(d-1)^{3(k-1)\ell}\widetilde{R}_{k}
. Here
\widetilde{R}_{k}
is a product of
k
factors, each taking one of the following:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-Q),\quad\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})},\quad(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)),\quad\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!].

Moreover,
\widetilde{R}_{k}
contains at least one factor of the form
\{\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-Q,\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}\}_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
(arising from
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
). Otherwise, the terms from the difference in (
7.21
) cancel out.

For each
\widetilde{R}_{k}
terms, we get
R_{k}
by replacing
\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-Q,\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
with
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q,G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
, respectively. As
k\geqslant 2
and each factor of
R_{k},\widetilde{R}_{k}
is bounded by
N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
(by
Lemma

3.9
and the assumption that
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
), the replacement error is bounded by

\displaystyle|\widetilde{R}_{k}-R_{k}|\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{{\mathfrak{b}}/2}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|.

We collect the above error in
{\mathcal{E}}
(as in (
7.14
)).

We denote the
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the form
(d-1)^{3(k-1)\ell}R_{k}
as
{\mathcal{U}}
. This finishes the proof of
Lemma

7.4
.

∎

7.2
Proof of
Proposition

7.3

By plugging (
7.12
) into (
7.8
), we get the following four terms

\displaystyle\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)\right],

(7.26)

\displaystyle\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}\right],

(7.27)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega){\mathcal{U}}\right],

(7.28)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega){\mathcal{E}}\right].

(7.29)

First term (
7.26
).

The first term (
7.26
) is negligible

\displaystyle\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)\right]=\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]).

(7.30)

If we temporarily ignore the indicator and the averaging over embeddings, the
claim (
7.30
) reduces to the definition of
Q

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\in[\![{N}]\!]}A_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}\,(G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q)=0.

(7.31)

which follows from that
({\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}})
are identically distributed under
the local resampling. In (
7.30
), for the average over embeddings, we can first sum over the indices
b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}
using (
7.31
), and then average over other indices
{\bf i}\setminus\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}
.

Second term (
7.27
).

Assume the following estimate for the second term (
7.27
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}\right]\\
&=\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q)\right]+\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/4}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]).\end{split}

(7.32)

Then we can further change
(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)
in (
7.32
) to
(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)
and get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)\times(G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q))\right]\\
+&\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Y-Q||G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q)|\right].\end{split}

(7.33)

The first term on the righthand side of (
7.33
) is in the form of (
7.10
).
The second term on the righthand side of (
7.33
) can be further bounded as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\OO(1)}{(d-1)^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Y-Q|N^{-2{\mathfrak{b}}}\right]=\OO\left(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/4}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]\right).\end{split}

Next we outline the proof of (
7.32
). If we temporarily ignore the indicator and the averaging over embeddings, the
claim (
7.32
) boils down to compute the following quantity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\in[\![{N}]\!]}A_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}A_{b_{\beta}c_{\beta}}G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}.

(7.34)

For random
d
-regular graphs, the adjacency matrices have a trivial eigenvector
(1,1,\cdots,1)^{\top}
. As a consequence the row and column sums of the Green’s function is small:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[\![{N}]\!]}G_{ij}=\sum_{j\in[\![{N}]\!]}G_{ij}=1/(d/\sqrt{d-1}-z)=\OO(1).

(7.35)

Thus if
G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}
in (
7.34
) is replaced by
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}
(without removing the vertices
b_{\alpha},b_{\beta}
), then the average is very small
\OO(1/N)
. To compute (
7.34
), we need to express
G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}
back to
G^{(b_{\alpha})},G^{(b_{\beta})},G
using Schur complement formula by carefully adding vertices
b_{\alpha},b_{\beta}
back. And it turns out we have nice leading term, and all other terms are negligible

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\in[\![{N}]\!]}A_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}A_{b_{\beta}c_{\beta}}G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}\\
&=\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\in[\![{N}]\!]}A_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}A_{b_{\beta}c_{\beta}}\frac{G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}}{d-1}(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q)+\text{``negligible error"}.\end{split}

(7.36)

The claim (
7.32
) follows by first summing over the indices
b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}
using (
7.36
), and then averaging over the remaining indices in
{\bf i}\setminus\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\}
.

Proof of (
7.36
).

For simplicity of notation, we write
b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}
as
b,c,b^{\prime},c^{\prime}
.
First we notice that using (
7.35
), the average of Green’s functions with only one vertex removed is also small. More precisely, the Schur complement formula (
2.5
) gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{c\sim b}\left(G_{cc^{\prime}}-\frac{G_{cb^{\prime}}G_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}}\right)=\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),\quad\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c}G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}=\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),\end{split}

(7.37)

where in the first statement we average over the free index
c
; in the second statement we average over the free index
b
.

To show (
7.36
), thanks to the Schur complement formula (
2.5
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}=G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}+(G^{(bb^{\prime})}H)_{cb}G^{(b^{\prime})}_{bc^{\prime}}=G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}+\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\sum_{x\sim b}G_{cx}^{(bb^{\prime})}\\
&=G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}+\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\sum_{x\sim b}\left(G_{cx}^{(b)}-\frac{G_{cb^{\prime}}^{(b)}G_{b^{\prime}x}^{(b)}}{G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}^{(b)}}\right)=G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}+\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\sum_{x\sim b}G_{cx}^{(b)}-\sum_{x\sim b}\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}G_{cb^{\prime}}^{(b)}G_{b^{\prime}x}^{(b)}}{\sqrt{d-1}G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}^{(b)}}.\end{split}

(7.38)

If we average over the edges
(b,c)
, the first term on the righthand side of (
7.38
) is small by (
7.37
); for the second term, by the same reasoning, we can replace
\sum_{x\sim b}G_{cx}^{(b)}
by
\sum_{x\sim b}(G_{cx}^{(b)}-Q)
; for the last term, we can bound it by the Ward identity:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c}\sum_{x\sim b}\left|\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}G_{cb^{\prime}}^{(b)}G_{b^{\prime}x}^{(b)}}{G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}^{(b)}}\right|&\lesssim\frac{A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}}{N}\sum_{b\sim c}|G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}G_{cb^{\prime}}^{(b)}|\\
&\lesssim\frac{A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}}{N}\sum_{b\sim c}(|G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}|^{2}+|G_{cb^{\prime}}^{(b)}|^{2})\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\Phi,\end{split}

(7.39)

where in the first statement we used that
|G_{b^{\prime}x}^{(b)}|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
for
b\sim x
from (
3.26
); in the second statement we used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality; and in the last statement we used (
3.26
).

Thus averaging over the edges
(b,c)
for (
7.38
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}&=\frac{A_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{Nd}\sum_{b\sim c}\left(\frac{G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\left(\sum_{x\sim b}G_{cx}^{(b)}-Q)\right)+\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\Phi)\right).\end{split}

(7.40)

We can then average over edges
(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
in (
7.40
). Similar to (
7.38
), we can first replace
G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}=G_{bc^{\prime}}+G_{bb^{\prime}}(HG^{(b^{\prime})})_{b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}=G_{bc^{\prime}}+\frac{G_{bb^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{d-1}}\sum_{x\sim b^{\prime}}G^{(b^{\prime})}_{c^{\prime}x},\end{split}

(7.41)

and conclude

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b\sim c\atop b^{\prime}\sim c^{\prime}}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}&=\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b\sim c\atop b^{\prime}\sim c^{\prime}}\frac{G_{bb^{\prime}}}{d-1}\left(\sum_{x\sim b}G_{cx}^{(b)}-Q\right)\left(\sum_{x\sim b^{\prime}}G_{c^{\prime}x}^{(b^{\prime})}-Q\right)+\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\Phi)\\
&=\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b\sim c\atop b^{\prime}\sim c^{\prime}}\frac{G_{bb^{\prime}}}{d-1}(G_{cc}^{(b)}-Q)(G_{c^{\prime}c^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}-Q)\\
&+\frac{N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b\sim c\atop b^{\prime}\sim c^{\prime}}\OO\left(|G_{bb^{\prime}}|\left(\sum_{x\sim b,x\neq c}|G_{cx}^{(b)}|+\sum_{x\sim b^{\prime},x\neq c^{\prime}}|G_{c^{\prime}x}^{(b^{\prime})}|\right)+\Phi\right),\end{split}

where for the last equality we bound
|G_{cx}^{(b)}-\delta_{cx}Q|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
for
x\sim b
, and
|G_{c^{\prime}x}^{(b^{\prime})}-\delta_{c^{\prime}x}Q|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}
for
x\sim b^{\prime}
using (
3.26
) and (
3.21
). This gives (
7.36
).
∎

Third term (
7.28
).

We recall the first few terms of
{\mathcal{U}}
from (
7.15
).
We have the following decomposition for the third term in (
7.28
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega){\mathcal{U}}\right]=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3},

(7.42)

where

\displaystyle J_{1}=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)L^{(i)}_{l_{\alpha}l_{\alpha}}}{(d-1)^{\ell+2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)^{2}\right],

(7.43)

\displaystyle J_{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i},\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\atop\alpha\neq\beta}\sum_{{\bf i}^{+}}\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)(L^{(i)}_{l_{\beta}l_{\beta}}+L^{(i)}_{l_{\alpha}l_{\beta}})}{(d-1)^{\ell+2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})})^{2}\right].

(7.44)

And
J_{3}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the form

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{(d-1)^{3(h-1)}}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)R_{h}\right],\quad h\geqslant 2,

(7.45)

where
R_{h}
is a product of
h
terms in the form (
7.11
), which contains
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
or
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
. Moreover, either
h\geqslant 3
, or
h=2
and
R_{h}
(recall from (
7.15
)) is one of the following terms

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)(G_{c_{\beta}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\beta})}-Q),\quad(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)G_{c_{\alpha^{\prime}}c_{\beta^{\prime}}}^{(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}b_{\beta^{\prime}})},\quad G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}G_{c_{\alpha^{\prime}}c_{\beta^{\prime}}}^{(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}b_{\beta^{\prime}})},\\
&\{G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q,G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}\}\times(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)),\end{split}

(7.46)

where
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!],\alpha^{\prime}\neq\beta^{\prime}\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\}
.

In the following we discuss the three terms
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
one by one. For
J_{1}
, by the same argument as in (
7.33
), we can change one copy of
(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)
in (
7.43
) to
(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)
, and the error is bounded by
\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi])
. After such replacement, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)L^{(i)}_{l_{\alpha}l_{\alpha}}}{(d-1)^{\ell+2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q)\right],

(7.47)

which is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms in the form (
7.9
) with
{\mathfrak{q}}=0
.

For
J_{2}
as in (
7.44
), we claim the following estimate

\displaystyle|J_{2}|\;\lesssim\;\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta}\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\ell}\,Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\,\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\,\big|G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}\big|^{2}\right]\;\lesssim\;(d-1)^{2\ell}\,\mathbb{E}[\Psi],

(7.48)

so this contribution can be absorbed into the error term
{\mathcal{E}}
in
(
7.8
).

If we temporarily ignore the indicator and the averaging over embeddings, the
claim (
7.48
) boils down to compute the following quantity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\in[\![{N}]\!]}A_{c_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}A_{b_{\beta}c_{\beta}}|G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|^{2}\lesssim\Phi.

(7.49)

Proof of (
7.49
).

Averages like (
7.49
) can be bounded by the classical Ward identity (
2.6
), which gives

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\big|G_{ij}(z)\big|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}m_{N}(z)}{N\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]}\lesssim\Phi(z).

(7.50)

However, the entry

G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}
(the Green’s function with rows/columns

b_{\alpha},b_{\beta}
removed) depends on the choice of these edges, but we can
express it in terms of the full Green’s function
G
by the Schur complement
formula and bound the average using the Ward identity. For simplicity of notation, we write
b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}
as
b,c,b^{\prime},c^{\prime}
.

We start with the Schur complement formula (
2.4
)

\displaystyle G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}=G_{cc^{\prime}}-(G(G|_{\{bb^{\prime}\}})^{-1}G)_{cc^{\prime}},\quad(G|_{\{bb^{\prime}\}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{G_{bb}G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}-G_{bb^{\prime}}^{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}G_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}&-G_{bb^{\prime}}\\
-G_{bb^{\prime}}&G_{bb}\end{array}\right].

Thanks to (
3.21
), the Green’s function terms are bounded, and the denominators in (
7.2
) are bounded away from
0
when
b,b^{\prime}
are far from each other, we can get

\displaystyle|G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}|\lesssim|G_{bb^{\prime}}|+|G_{bc^{\prime}}|+|G_{b^{\prime}c}|+|G_{cc^{\prime}}|.

(7.53)

Then the Ward identity bound (
7.50
) leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{c\sim b\atop c^{\prime}\sim b^{\prime}}|G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{(Nd)^{2}}\sum_{c\sim b\atop c^{\prime}\sim b^{\prime}}(|G_{bb^{\prime}}|^{2}+|G_{bc^{\prime}}|^{2}+|G_{b^{\prime}c}|^{2}+|G_{cc^{\prime}}|^{2})\lesssim\Phi.\end{split}

(7.54)

∎

Finally, for
J_{3}
, if
h=2
,
R_{h}
is given in (
7.46
). There are two cases

1.

R_{h}
contains a factor
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
, then we can first average over edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
as in (
7.31
). In this case we can bound (
7.45
) by the same way as for (
7.30
), and it is bounded by
\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi])

2.

R_{h}
contains a factor
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
, then we can first average over edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}),(b_{\beta},c_{\beta})
as in (
7.34
). In this case we can estimate (
7.45
) by the same way as for (
7.32
), and get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{(d-1)}\frac{(d-1)^{3(h+1)\ell}}{(d-1)^{{\mathfrak{q}}\ell/2}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(G^{(b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Y)R^{\prime}_{h+1}\right]+\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/4}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]),\end{split}

(7.55)

where
R^{\prime}_{h+1}
is obtained from
R_{h}
by replacing
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
by
G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q)
, and it contains
h+1
factors in the form (
7.11
); and
{\mathfrak{q}}=12
. This leads to (
7.10
).

If
h\geqslant 3
, we recall that
R_{h}
contains either
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
or
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
. There are three cases

1.

If
R_{h}
contains a factor
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
, we can change it to
(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)
, and the error is bounded by
\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi])
.

2.

If
R_{h}
contains two factors in the form
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and similarly to (
7.49
), we can bound it as

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}\frac{(d-1)^{3(h-1)}}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)N^{-(h-2){\mathfrak{b}}}|G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}|^{2}\right]=\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi]).

3.

If
R_{h}
does not contain any factor in the form
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q
, contains exactly one factor in the form
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
and all other factors are
Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)
, then by the same reasoning as in (
7.55
), up to negligible error we can replace
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}
by
(G^{(b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Y)\times(G_{b_{\alpha}b_{{\beta}}}(G_{c_{{\beta}}c_{{\beta}}}^{(b_{{\beta}})}-Q))
.

All three cases above lead to (
7.10
).

Fourth term (
7.29
).

We recall
{\mathcal{E}}
from (
7.14
), the last term (
7.29
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega){\mathcal{E}}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)\left(\frac{m_{\rm sc}^{2(\ell+1)}(z)}{(d-1)^{\ell+1}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}})\right)\right]\\
&+\OO\left(\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)\left(\frac{1}{N^{{\mathfrak{b}}/2}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\right]\right).\end{split}

(7.56)

We can express
{\widetilde{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}
in terms of the Green’s function
G
of the original graph using Schur complement formula. In
Lemma

13.3
we show

\displaystyle{\widetilde{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}=G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}+{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha\beta},

(7.57)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha\beta}|&\lesssim\sum_{\gamma\in[\![{\mu}]\!],x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}}(|G_{c_{\alpha}x}^{(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{W})}|^{2}+|G_{c_{\beta}x}^{(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{W})}|^{2})+\sum_{\gamma\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\setminus\{\alpha,\beta\}}(|G^{(\mathbb{T}b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}b_{\gamma}}|^{2}+|G^{(\mathbb{T}b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\beta}b_{\gamma}}|^{2})\\
&+\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}}(|G_{c_{\alpha}x}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}|^{2}+|G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\beta}x}|^{2})+N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}\Phi,\end{split}

(7.58)

and
N_{\gamma}=\{x\neq c_{\gamma}:x\sim b_{\gamma}\}\cup\{a_{\gamma}\}
.

After averaging over embeddings of
{\mathcal{F}}
(i.e., over
{\bf i}
) most sums in
(
7.58
) are controlled by (
7.50
). For instance, averaging
over
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
and
(b_{\gamma},c_{\gamma})
bounds

\mathbb{E}\big[|G^{(\mathbb{T}b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}b_{\gamma}}|^{2}\big]
via Ward identity.

The delicate case is terms such as
|G^{(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{W})}_{c_{\alpha}x}|^{2}
with

x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
, where
x
and
c_{\alpha}
are graph–neighbors of a common
vertex and the embedding average does not decouple their indices. To handle
these, we introduce a new Ward-type estimate, which we call the

punctured-vertex Ward bound
:
if
i,j
are two neighbors of a vertex
o
(so
o\sim i
and
o\sim j
), then

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\,\big|G^{(o)}_{ij}(z)\big|^{2}\,\right]\;\leqslant\;N^{{\mathfrak{o}}}\,\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}m_{N}(z)]}{N\eta},

(7.59)

proved in Lemma
13.1
. The idea is to perform local
resampling at
o
, use the invariance

\mathbb{E}[|G^{(o)}_{ij}|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{G}^{(o)}_{ij}|^{2}]
. We then expand
\widetilde{G}_{ij}^{(o)}(z)
using the Schur complement formula. Crucially, we can bound
\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{G}_{ij}^{(o)}(z)|^{2}]
, by
\mathbb{E}[|G_{ij}^{(o)}(z)|^{2}]
times a small factor, and errors as in (
7.50
), leading to the desired bound given by the right-hand side of (
7.59
).

Combining (
7.57
), (
7.58
) with Ward’s identity
(
7.50
) and the bound (
7.59
), we obtain

\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\,\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}}\big(\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}\big)\right]=\OO\big((d-1)^{2\ell}\,\mathbb{E}[\Psi]\big),

and the second line in (
7.56
) is

\OO\!\big(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/4}\,\mathbb{E}[\Psi]\big)
. See
Section

13
for the full
statement and proof.

8
Switching edges, the forest and admissible functions

Figure 6
:
Top Panel:
In the forest
\mathcal{F}
, red edges represent core edges
\mathcal{C}
. The used core edges belong to radius-
(\ell+1)
balls, while each unused core edge
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
forms its own connected component. Together, the red and green edges constitute the switching edges
\mathcal{K}
. Bottom Panel: We construct
\mathcal{F}^{+}
from
\mathcal{F}
by selecting an unused core edge (the rightmost red edge), expanding it into a radius-
(\ell+1)
ball, and adding
\mu
new switching edges.

We recall from
Proposition

7.3
that, up to negligible errors,

\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(Q-Y)]

decomposes into an
\OO(1)
–weighted sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\,\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\,(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Y)\,R_{\bf i}\bigr],

(8.1)

where
{\mathcal{F}}
from (
7.4
) contains all switching edges, and

R_{\bf i}
is a product of factors of the form (
7.11
).
To estimate (
8.1
), we perform a local resampling around
the edge
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
and repeat the procedure from

Section

7
, which naturally leads to an iterative scheme.

At each iteration, we locally resample the graph and express the
Green’s function of the switched graph in terms of the original one as in
Section

7
.
Because almost all neighborhoods in
{\mathcal{G}}
are acyclic, with high
probability the edges involved in these resamplings have large tree
neighborhoods and are typically far apart. Hence, we may regard them
collectively as forming a forest. In this section, we formalize this
iterative procedure using a sequence of forests (see

Figure

6
), which encode all edges involved in local
resamplings.

Fix
\mu:=d(d-1)^{\ell}
, the number of boundary edges of the

d
–regular tree truncated at depth
\ell+1
.
We begin with a two–vertex
template
(an unlabeled graph):

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}:=(V_{0},E_{0}),\qquad V_{0}=\{\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0}\},\quad E_{0}=\{(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0})\}.

Here
\mathtt{i}_{0}
and
\mathtt{o}_{0}
are formal symbols
(distinguished template vertices) that become concrete vertices of

{\mathcal{G}}
only once an embedding of
{\mathcal{F}}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
is specified.

Initialization.

Construct
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
from
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
as follows:

•

extend the directed edge
(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0})
to a
truncated
d
–regular tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{0})
rooted at

\mathtt{o}_{0}
;

•

add
\mu
boundary edges

{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{1}=\{e^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
to

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{0})
to obtain

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{0})
;

•

introduce
\mu
new directed edges

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.

Thus,

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}={\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{0})\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1},\qquad{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{0})\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{1}={\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{0}).

(8.2)

The subgraph
{\mathcal{F}}
in (
7.4
) can be viewed as an embedding
of
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
.

General step.

Given
{\mathcal{F}}_{s}=(V_{s},E_{s})
and the sets
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{s},{\mathcal{M}}_{s}
created at the
previous steps, construct
{\mathcal{F}}_{s+1}
by

•

choosing a (previously created) core edge

e=(\mathtt{i}_{s},\mathtt{o}_{s})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{s}
and extending it to

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{s})
;

•

adding
\mu
boundary edges

{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{s+1}=\{e^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
to get

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{s})
;

•

adding
\mu
new directed edges

{\mathcal{M}}_{s+1}=\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.

Explicitly,

{\mathcal{F}}_{s+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{s}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{s})\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{s+1},\quad{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{s})\,\cup\,{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{s+1}={\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}_{s}).

(8.3)

We refer to
Figure

6
for the construction of
{\mathcal{F}}_{s+1}={\mathcal{F}}^{+}
from
{\mathcal{F}}_{s}={\mathcal{F}}
.

Embedding into
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Local resampling (Section
5
) yields an embedding
of
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{s+1}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Under an embedding we write

{\mathcal{F}}_{s}={\mathcal{F}}_{{\bf i}_{s}}
with vertex tuple

{\bf i}_{s}\in[\![{N}]\!]^{|V_{s}|}
;

\mathtt{i}_{s}\mapsto i_{s}
and
\mathtt{o}_{s}\mapsto o_{s}
.
The neighborhood
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}_{s})
maps to the ball

B_{\ell}(o_{s};{\mathcal{G}})
; boundary edges
e^{\prime}_{\alpha},e_{\alpha}
map to

(\ell_{\alpha},a_{\alpha})
and
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Core and switching edges.

Let

{\mathcal{K}}_{s}:=\{(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0})\}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{s}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{s}

be the set of switching edges, and

{\mathcal{C}}_{s}:=\{(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0})\}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{s}

the core edges (each component of
{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
contains exactly one core edge).
Write the used core edges as

\{(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0}),\dots,(\mathtt{i}_{s-1},\mathtt{o}_{s-1})\}
and

{\mathcal{C}}_{s}^{\circ}:={\mathcal{C}}_{s}\setminus\{(\mathtt{i}_{0},\mathtt{o}_{0}),\dots,(\mathtt{i}_{s-1},\mathtt{o}_{s-1})\}

for the unused ones.

For most arguments we freeze a step and abbreviate

{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}={\mathcal{C}}_{s}^{\circ},\qquad{\mathcal{F}}^{+}={\mathcal{F}}_{s+1},\ {\mathcal{K}}^{+}={\mathcal{K}}_{s+1},\ {\mathcal{C}}^{+}={\mathcal{C}}_{s+1},\ ({\mathcal{C}}^{\circ})^{+}={\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}_{s+1}.

In general,

{\mathcal{F}}=\!\!\bigcup_{e\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}}\!\{e\}\ \cup\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathtt{i}^{\prime},\mathtt{o}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}}\!\!\!\!{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}^{\prime}),\qquad{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{C}}\ \cup\ \!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathtt{i}^{\prime},\mathtt{o}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}}\!\!\!\!\big({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o}^{\prime})\setminus{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o}^{\prime})\big).

(8.4)

If we expand an unused core edge
(\mathtt{i},\mathtt{o})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
by

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o})={\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(\mathtt{o})\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, where

{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{e^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, and add

{\mathcal{M}}=\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, then

{\mathcal{C}}^{+}={\mathcal{C}}\cup{\mathcal{M}},\qquad({\mathcal{C}}^{\circ})^{+}={\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}\cup{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\{(\mathtt{i},\mathtt{o})\},\qquad{\mathcal{F}}^{+}={\mathcal{F}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell+1}(\mathtt{o})\cup{\mathcal{M}},\qquad{\mathcal{K}}^{+}={\mathcal{K}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}.

(8.5)

We refer to
Figure

6
for
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
.

Indicator for “good” embeddings.

For most of this paper we view
{\mathcal{F}}=(V,E)
as an embedded (labeled) copy of the
template in
{\mathcal{G}}
; this amounts to choosing a vertex assignment

{\bf i}\in[\![{N}]\!]^{|V|}
. Different choices of
{\bf i}

yield different embeddings. Under this convention,
{\mathcal{K}}
,
{\mathcal{C}}
, and

{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
are also regarded as embedded subgraphs of
{\mathcal{G}}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we write
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
to emphasize that we
have fixed an embedding of
{\mathcal{F}}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
. As discussed
above, one local resampling step produces a new embedded forest

{\mathcal{F}}^{+}={\mathcal{F}}^{+}({{\bf i}^{+}},E^{+})\subset{\mathcal{G}}
with vertex tuple
{\bf i}^{+}
.

The forest
{\mathcal{F}}
records the edges involved in all previous local resamplings.
To ensure these edges are well separated and lie in large tree neighborhoods, we
use the following indicator.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
(as in (
8.4
)) with core edges
{\mathcal{C}}
, viewed as a
subgraph of a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
with adjacency matrix

A
. Define
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
if and only if:
(i)
{\mathcal{F}}
is embedded in
{\mathcal{G}}
; (ii) for every
(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}
and every

x\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c;{\mathcal{G}})
, the ball
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(x;{\mathcal{G}})
is a tree; and
(iii) distinct core edges are at pairwise distance at least
3{\mathfrak{R}}
.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}):=\prod_{\{x,y\}\in E}A_{xy}\;\prod_{(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}}\;\prod_{x\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c;{\mathcal{G}})}\bm{1}\!\big({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(x;{\mathcal{G}})\ \text{is a tree}\big)\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,c),(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}\\
(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\bm{1}\!\big(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(c,c^{\prime})\geq 3{\mathfrak{R}}\big).

(8.6)

Fix a good embedding of
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
into
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
with
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
. Then we perform a local resampling around an unused core edge
(i,o)
.
In the following lemma, we show that with high probability with respect to the randomness of
{\bf S}
, the randomly selected edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
are far away from each other, and have large tree neighborhood. In particular
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Fix a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, and a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
(as in (
8.4
)) viewed as a subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Assume that
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
and
|{\bf i}|\leqslant N^{{\mathfrak{c}}/2}
.
We consider the local resampling around an unused core edge
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
, with resampling data
\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
We denote the set of resampling data
{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})\subset{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
(recall from
Section

5
) such that the following holds

1.

for any
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}\cup{\bf i},\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})\geqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}}
;

2.

for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},{\mathcal{G}})
, the radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhood of
v
is a tree.

Then
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}({\sf F}({\mathcal{G}}))\geqslant 1-N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}}
(where
\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}(\cdot)
is the probability with respect to the randomness of
{\bf S}
as in
Definition

5.3
). Also, for
{\bf S}\in{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})
the following holds

1.

\mu=d(d-1)^{\ell}
,
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}=[\![{\mu}]\!]
(recall from (
5.2
)), and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}=T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}})\in\overline{\Omega}
;

2.

I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
and
I({\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})=1
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.2
.

We sequentially select
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
uniformly random from
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
. For any fixed
\alpha
, we consider all edges that would break the requirements of the lemma. For the first requirement, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}({\bf i}\cup_{1\leqslant\beta\leqslant\alpha-1}\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\},\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\})\leqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}})\lesssim N^{-1}(|{\bf i}|+2\mu)d(d-1)^{3{\mathfrak{R}}}\leqslant N^{-1+3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}.

(8.7)

For the second requirement, we recall that
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, in which all vertices except for
N^{\mathfrak{c}}
many have radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{R}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is not a tree for some }v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}))\leqslant N^{-1}N^{\mathfrak{c}}d(d-1)^{\ell}\leqslant N^{-1+3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}.

(8.8)

The claim
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}({\sf F}({\mathcal{G}}))\geqslant 1-N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}}
follows from union bounding over all
\alpha
using (
8.7
) and (
8.8
).

Under our assumption
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
, the radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhood of
o
is a tree. Thus
\mu=d(d-1)^{\ell}
. Moreover, the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{B}}_{3{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
, and
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}})
for
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
are disjoint. It follows that
\dist_{{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},\{a_{\beta},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})>{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}
for all
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
, and the subgraph
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})})
after adding the edge
\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\}
is a tree for all
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
. We conclude that
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}=[\![{\mu}]\!]
.

Next we show that for any vertex
v\in[\![{N}]\!]
, the excess of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is no bigger than that of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
. Then it follows that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\overline{\Omega}
.
If
\dist(v,\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]})\geqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})={\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
, and the statement follows.
Otherwise either
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{a_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},{\mathcal{G}})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{3{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
or
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}})
for some
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
. We will discuss the first case. The second case can be proven in the same way, so we omit its proof.
If
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
, we denote
r:=\min_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(v,\{l_{\alpha}\})\leqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
. Then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a subgraph of

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\cup_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
after removing
\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
and adding
\{(l_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
. By our construction of
{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
are disjoint trees. We conclude that
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a tree.
If
v\not\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
,
we denote
r:=\min_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(v,a_{\alpha})\leqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a subgraph of

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\cup_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})

after removing
\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
and adding
\{(a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
Again by our construction of
{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
are disjoint trees, we conclude the excess of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is at most that of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
.

The claim
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
follows from the construction of
{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})
. It also follows from the above discussion that
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{R}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a tree for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
. One can then check that
I({\mathcal{F}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})=1
. This finishes the proof of the second statement in
Lemma

8.2
.

∎

Number of “good” embeddings.

From (
8.4
), each connected component of
{\mathcal{F}}
is either an unused core edge or a radius-
(\ell+1)
ball corresponding to a used core edge. The following proposition states that the total number of embeddings where
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
is approximately equal to that of choosing each connected component independently.

Proposition 8.3
.

Given a template
{\mathcal{F}}=(V,E)
with core edges
{\mathcal{C}}
and unused core edges
{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
as in (
8.4
), as well as a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, we have

\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-2{\mathfrak{c}}}}\right)\right),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}:=(Nd)^{|{\mathcal{C}}|}\left([(d-1)!]^{1+d+d(d-1)+\cdots+d(d-1)^{\ell-1}}\right)^{|{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}|}.\end{split}

(8.9)

Here
|{\mathcal{C}}|
is the number of core edges; and
|{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}|
is the number of used core edges. We remark that
Z_{\mathcal{F}}
depends only on the template
{\mathcal{F}}
but not
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Remark 8.4
.

In the rest of this article, we have many expressions in the following form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{\bf i}\mathbb{E}[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\times(\cdots)].

(8.10)

Thanks to
Proposition

8.3
, the above expression can be viewed as an average over all possible embedding of the template
{\mathcal{F}}
into
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

We notice that
|{\mathcal{C}}|
is also the number of connected components of
{\mathcal{F}}
, and
|{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}|
is the number of connected components in
{\mathcal{F}}
which are balls of radius
\ell+1
.

We can prove (
8.9
) by
induction on the number of connected components. If
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of a single edge
{\mathcal{F}}=\{b,c\}
which is an unused core edge, then

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=\sum_{b,c}A_{bc}\prod_{v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c,{\mathcal{G}})}\bm{1}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is a tree}))=Nd\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}}\right)\right),

(8.11)

where we used the definition of
\overline{\Omega}
from
Definition

3.1
.
If
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of a radius
(\ell+1)
-ball, corresponding to one used core edge, then we can also first sum over its core edge. The number of choices of this is the same as (
8.11
). Then we sum over the remaining vertices. Each interior vertex of the radius-
(\ell+1)
ball contributes a factor
(d-1)!
, since there are
(d-1)!
ways to embed its children vertices. We get

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=Nd[(d-1)!]^{1+d+d(d-1)+\cdots+d(d-1)^{\ell-1}}\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}}\right)\right).

(8.12)

If the statement holds for
{\mathcal{F}}
with
\theta
connected components, next we show it for
{\mathcal{F}}
with
\theta+1
connected components. We can first sum over the indices corresponding to a connected component, fixing the other indices.
If it is a single edge, we get a factor similar to (
8.11
); if it is a radius-
(\ell+1)
ball, we get a factor similar to (
8.12
). Next we can sum over the remaining
\theta
connected components of
{\mathcal{F}}
, which gives (
8.9
).

∎

8.1
Admissible functions

Later, we repeatedly localize the Green’s function on the embedded forest

{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
and separate the
tree-like
main term from the

fluctuation
. Concretely, for vertices
w,w^{\prime}\in{\bf i}
we approximate the
global Green’s function entry
G_{ww^{\prime}}(z)
by a deterministic local kernel

L_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
, which coincides with the Green’s function on copies of the

d
-regular tree whenever the neighborhood of
{\mathcal{F}}
is cycle-free. The error

G^{\circ}:=G-L
will be treated perturbatively. To organize the resulting
expressions, we introduce a class of
admissible functions
, namely products
of resolvent factors associated with core and switching edges.

Definition 8.5

(Local Green’s Function)
.

Given a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
embedded in
{\mathcal{G}}
, we introduce the local Green’s function
L(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
: for
w,w^{\prime}\in{\bf i}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&L_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=P_{ww^{\prime}}({\mathcal{F}},z,m_{\rm sc}(z)).\end{split}

(8.13)

Given the event
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
,
L_{ww^{\prime}}
is simply the Green’s function of copies of
d
-regular trees (recall from
Proposition

3.3
):
L_{ww^{\prime}}=0
if
w,w^{\prime}
are disconnected in
{\mathcal{F}}
, otherwise,

\displaystyle L_{ww^{\prime}}=m_{d}(z)\left(-\frac{m_{\rm sc}(z)}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{{\mathcal{F}}}(w,w^{\prime})}.

We also denote the centered version of the Green’s function as

\displaystyle G_{ww^{\prime}}^{\circ}(z)=G_{ww^{\prime}}(z)-L_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}).

(8.14)

When the context is clear, we will simply write
G^{\circ}(z),L(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
as
G^{\circ},L
for simplicity.

Remark 8.6
.

At each step, we expand the forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
to a new forest
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}=({\bf i}^{+},E^{+})
by including local resampling data (
{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
are embedded subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}
). Given the event
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
, the local Green’s functions are compatible (both are given by the Green’s function of copies of
d
-regular trees)

\displaystyle L_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=L_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}}),\quad w,w^{\prime}\in{\bf i}.

Later, we need the local Green’s function with one vertex removed: Let
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
, and recall
P^{(i)}
from (
3.5
),

\displaystyle L^{(i)}_{ww^{\prime}}:=L^{(i)}_{ww^{\prime}}(z,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}):=P^{(i)}_{ww^{\prime}}({\mathcal{F}},z,m_{\rm sc}(z)).

(8.15)

Definition 8.7

(Admissible Function)
.

Consider a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
as defined in (
8.4
), with switching edges
{\mathcal{K}}
, core edges
{\mathcal{C}}
, unused core edges
{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
. For any nonnegative integers
r\geqslant 0
, we denote the set of admissible functions
\Adm(r,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
where a function
R_{{\bf i}}\in\Adm(r,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
contains
r
factors of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\{(G_{cc}^{(b)}-Q)\}_{(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}},\quad\{G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})},G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})},G_{bb^{\prime}}\}_{(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}},\quad\{G^{\circ}_{ss^{\prime}}\}_{s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}},\quad(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)).\end{split}

(8.16)

Remark 8.8
.

At each step, we expand the forest
{\mathcal{F}}
to a new forest
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
by including local resampling data. This change also affects the admissible set of functions, which now expands as follows:

\displaystyle\Adm(r,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\subset\Adm(r,{\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}}).

We now give the general ways of bounding the terms involved in the admissible functions (recall from
Definition

8.7
).

Proposition 8.9
.

We take
z\in{\bf D}
(recall from (
1.8
)). Then the following holds:

1.

For any factor
B
in (
8.16
), we have

\displaystyle\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})|B|\lesssim N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}}.

(8.17)

2.

Let
R_{\bf i}\in\Adm(r,{\bm{r}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
as in
Definition

8.7
.
If
R_{\bf i}
contains two terms of the form
\{G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})},G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})},G_{bb^{\prime}}\}
with
(c,b)\neq(c^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{(d-1)^{3\ell r}}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})R_{\bf i}=\OO\left(\frac{(d-1)^{3\ell r}N^{\mathfrak{o}}}{N^{(r-2){\mathfrak{b}}}}\Phi\right).

(8.18)

If we further assume
r\geqslant 3
, then the expectation of (
8.18
) is bounded by
\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{b}}/2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{p}])
;

Proof of
Proposition

8.9
.

The claim (
8.17
) follows from the definition (
3.21
) of the set
\Omega
and can be proved in the same way as
Lemma

3.9
. Thus, we omit its proof.
For (
8.18
), we show the case that
R_{\bf i}
contains
(G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})})^{2}
. The other cases can be proven in the same way so we omit. We can first bound the other factors of
R_{\bf i}
using (
8.17
), as
N^{-(r-2){\mathfrak{b}}}
. Then (
8.18
) reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{(d-1)^{3\ell r}}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})|R_{\bf i}|\lesssim\frac{(d-1)^{3\ell r}}{N^{(r-2){\mathfrak{b}}}Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})|G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{(d-1)^{3\ell r}\Phi}{N^{(r-2){\mathfrak{b}}}Z_{\mathcal{F}}},

(8.19)

which follows from the same argument as (
7.49
).
∎

9
Switching using
the Schur complement formula

In this section we will use the Schur complement formula to study the Green’s function after local resampling.
We recall the local resampling and related notation from
Section

5
. We also introduce the following S-Product term.

Definition 9.1

(S-Product term)
.

Fix
r\geqslant 0
, we define
R_{r}
to be a
S-product term
of order
r
(where “
S
” indicates that these terms arise from expansions using the Schur complement formula) if it is a product of
r
factors in the following forms:

\displaystyle(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q),\quad G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})},\quad(Q-m_{\rm sc}(z)),\quad\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!].

In the following proposition, we derive an expansion for factors that are Green’s function entries with at most one index in
\{i,o\}
. They can be proven in exactly the same way as
Lemma

7.4
using the Schur complement formula, so we omit their proofs.

Proposition 9.2
.

We assume that
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
and
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
(recall from (
8.6
)), and define the index set
{\mathsf{A}}_{i}:=\{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]:\dist_{{\mathcal{T}}}(i,l_{\alpha})=\ell+1\}
(see
Figure

5
). Then for any unused core edges
(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}\setminus\{(i,o)\}
, the following holds:

1.

\widetilde{G}^{(ib)}_{oc}
and
\widetilde{G}^{(b)}_{ic}
can be rewritten as a weighted sum

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}(\bm{1}(\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}))G_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(b_{\alpha}b)}+{\mathcal{U}}+{\mathcal{E}},

(9.1)

where
|{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}(\cdot)|\lesssim 1
;
{\mathcal{U}}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms of the form
(d-1)^{3r\ell}R_{r}G_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(b_{\alpha}b)}
, for
R_{r}
an S-product term (see
Definition

9.1
) with
r\geqslant 1
, and the error
{\mathcal{E}}
is bounded by

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(\mathbb{T}b)}-G_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(b_{\alpha}b)}|+\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|+N^{-2}.

(9.2)

2.

\widetilde{G}_{ib}
and
\widetilde{G}_{ob}^{(i)}
can be rewritten as a weighted sum

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}(\bm{1}(\alpha\in{\mathsf{A}}_{i}))G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}b}+{\mathcal{U}}+{\mathcal{E}},

where
|{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}(\cdot)|\lesssim 1
;
{\mathcal{U}}
is an
\OO(1)
-weighted sum of terms of the form
(d-1)^{3r\ell}R_{r}G_{c_{\alpha}b}^{(b_{\alpha})}
, where
R_{r}
is an S-product term (see
Definition

9.1
) with
r\geqslant 1
;
and the error
{\mathcal{E}}
is bounded by

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}b}^{(\mathbb{T})}-G_{c_{\alpha}b}^{(b_{\alpha})}|+\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}-G^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}|+N^{-2}.

3.

\widetilde{G}_{cc}^{(b)},\widetilde{G}_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})},\widetilde{G}_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}
and
\widetilde{G}_{bb^{\prime}}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{cc}^{(b)}=G_{cc}^{(b)}+{\mathcal{E}},\quad|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T}b)}_{cc}-G^{(b)}_{cc}|+(d-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(\mathbb{T}b)}|^{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}=G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}+{\mathcal{E}},\quad|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T}bb^{\prime})}_{cc^{\prime}}-G^{(bb^{\prime})}_{cc^{\prime}}|+(d-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}(|\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c}^{(\mathbb{T}bb^{\prime})}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{c^{\prime}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T}bb^{\prime})}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}=G_{bc^{\prime}}^{(b^{\prime})}+{\mathcal{E}},\quad|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T}b^{\prime})}_{bc^{\prime}}-G^{(b^{\prime})}_{bc^{\prime}}|+(d-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}(|\widetilde{G}_{bc_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T}b^{\prime})}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{c^{\prime}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T}b^{\prime})}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{bb^{\prime}}=G_{bb^{\prime}}+{\mathcal{E}},\quad|{\mathcal{E}}|\lesssim|\widetilde{G}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{bb^{\prime}}-G_{bb^{\prime}}|+(d-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}(|\widetilde{G}_{bc_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}|^{2}+|\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}c_{\alpha}}^{(\mathbb{T})}|^{2}).

10
Switching using the Woodbury formula

In this section, we introduce a novel expansion based on the Woodbury formula (
2.2
).
In the rest of this section, we assume that
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
(recall from (
8.6
)). Then the switching edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
are far away from each other, and have large tree neighborhood. Thanks to
Lemma

8.2
,
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
holds with high probability provided
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
.

We compare the normalized adjacency matrix of the switched graph to that of the original graph,
\widetilde{H}-H
.
We recall from (
5.5
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}-H=-\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha},\quad\xi_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d-1}}\left(\Delta_{l_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}}+\Delta_{b_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{l_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{a_{\alpha}b_{\alpha}}\right).

We denote the rank of this difference as
r=\OO((d-1)^{\ell})
, and rewrite

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}-H=UV^{\top},

where
U,V
are
N\times r
matrices, and their nonzero rows correspond to the vertices
\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
Then, the Woodbury formula (
2.2
) gives us

\displaystyle{\widetilde{G}}-G=(H-z+UV^{\top})^{-1}-(H-z)^{-1}=-GU(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}GU)^{-1}V^{\top}G.

(10.1)

We recall
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
as in (
8.3
), and denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
the switched version of it

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{+}:={\mathcal{F}}\cup{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\cup\bigcup_{\alpha=1}^{\mu}\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\},\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{+}:={\mathcal{F}}\cup{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\cup\bigcup_{\alpha=1}^{\mu}\{(l_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}),(a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha})\}.

We view
{\mathcal{F}}^{+},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
as subgraphs of
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
respectively. We will analyze (
10.1
) using local Green’s functions

\displaystyle L:=P({\mathcal{F}}^{+},z,m_{\rm sc}(z)),\quad\widetilde{L}:=P(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{+},z,m_{\rm sc}(z)),

(10.2)

as was defined in
Definition

3.4
. We remark that condition on
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
, both
L
and
\widetilde{L}
are simply the Green’s function of copies of
d
-regular trees.

Notice that when restricted to the vertex set of
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
(which contains the vertices
\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}^{-1}-L^{-1}=\widetilde{H}-H=-\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha}=UV^{\top}.

(10.3)

We can use the Woodbury formula on
L,\widetilde{L}
as well, giving

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}-L=-LU(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}V^{\top}L.

(10.4)

A crucial observation is that the quantity
-U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}V^{\top}
in (
10.1
) and (
10.4
) take very simple form.

Lemma 10.1
.

We introduce the following matrix
F
, which is nonzero on the vertex set
\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
,

\displaystyle F:=\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha}{\widetilde{L}}\xi_{\beta}.

(10.5)

Then

\displaystyle F=-U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}V^{\top}.

(10.6)

Proof of
Lemma

10.1
.

The nonzero rows of
U,V
are parametrized by
\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
By rearranging the above expression (
10.4
) (we view all the matrices as restricted on the vertex set of
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
), we get

\displaystyle L^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}L^{-1}-L^{-1}=-U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}V^{\top}.

(10.7)

We can reorganize (
10.7
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}-U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}V^{\top}=L^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}L^{-1}-L^{-1}=L^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}{\widetilde{L}}^{-1}+L^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})-L^{-1}\\
&=L^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})=(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1}){\widetilde{L}}(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})+{\widetilde{L}}^{-1}{\widetilde{L}}(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})\\
&=(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})+(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1}){\widetilde{L}}(L^{-1}-{\widetilde{L}}^{-1})=\sum_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\xi_{\alpha}{\widetilde{L}}\xi_{\beta}=F,\end{split}

(10.8)

where in the last statement we used (
10.3
).
∎

Our next lemma attempts to expand
{\widetilde{G}}-G
in terms of
{\widetilde{L}}-L
.

Lemma 10.2
.

We assume that
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
and
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
(recall from (
8.6
)), and recall
G^{\circ}=(G-L)
. Then we have:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{G}}-G=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}GF(G^{\circ}F)^{k}G,

(10.9)

Proof.

Thanks to (
3.21
), uniformly for
x,y\in\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
We can then expand (
10.1
) using the resolvent identity (
2.1
) and (
10.6
) to conclude that

\displaystyle{\widetilde{G}}-G

\displaystyle=-GU(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}GU)^{-1}V^{\top}G=-GU(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU+V^{\top}G^{\circ}U)^{-1}V^{\top}G

\displaystyle=-GU\left((\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}\sum_{k\geqslant 0}(-1)^{k}(V^{\top}G^{\circ}U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1})^{k}\right)V^{\top}G

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}(-1)^{k+1}GU(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1}(V^{\top}G^{\circ}U(\mathbb{I}+V^{\top}LU)^{-1})^{k}V^{\top}G

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}GF(G^{\circ}F)^{k}G.

This gives (
10.9
).
∎

Proposition 10.3
.

We assume that
{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
and
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
(recall from (
8.6
)). Then for
w,w^{\prime}\in[\![{N}]\!]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|({\widetilde{G}}-G)_{ww^{\prime}}|\leqslant(d-1)^{\ell}\sum_{x\in\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}}(|G_{wx}|^{2}+|G_{w^{\prime}x}|^{2}).\end{split}

(10.10)

As a consequence, we have the following bounds
